Notes

1:9 And it came to pass in those days: There are three different ways to understand this time reference:

âThose daysâ links 1:9 to âthe beginning of the gospelâ (1:1) since both happened âin those days,â implying that Jesusâ baptism is (a part of) the beginning of the gospel.

âAnd it came to passâ is a Hebrew idiom used to impart a biblical flavor to the text.64It does not imply anything about the specific timing of the story. Rather, since âcameâ but not âand it came to passâ was used to introduce John the Baptist (1:4),65the language here suggests some similarity between John and Jesus, but also lends a more authoritative and important note to the introduction to Jesus through the use of a biblical-sounding phrase.

âAnd it came to passâ signals a change in time, implying that there is not necessarily anyone but John and Jesus present at his baptism (that is, the crowds who were baptized are no longer present).

that Jesus came from Nazareth of Galilee: Nazareth had a few hundred residents; no public buildings have been found there, presumably because it was too small to have any. Nazareth is never mentioned in the HB, Josephus, or the Talmud, and this silence speaks to its obscurity. Mark probably included the phrase âof Galileeâ because Nazareth would otherwise not have been identifiable to the audience. This introduction is akin to saying that Jesus was âfrom Nowheresville.â66

Hostility to Galilean Jews was a trait shared by many Jews from Judea (see 14:70; John 1:46; 7:41, 52), âparticularly when any religious issue was at stake,â67 suggesting that the typical audience member would not have anticipated Jesus to be a religious figure with authority based on this introduction.

Mark presents no other background for Jesus. This may be because

the audience was already familiar with Jesusâ background, and Mark wanted to use the limited time to focus on other topics.

Mark may have been unfamiliar with Jesusâ earlier life.

Mark wanted to emphasize the theme of discipleship, which would neither require nor be advanced by an exploration of Jesusâ birth and youth; rather, introducing Jesus at his baptism (and, if he was in fact Johnâs disciple,68introducing him in relation to his teacher) emphasizes the theme of discipleship.

Mark may be alluding to Genesis 14:18 (which presents Melchizedek as an important figure with no background) and/or 1 Kings 17:1 (which presents Elijah as a great prophet with no background).

and was baptized of John in Jordan: The idea that Jesus would be baptized is surprising in this context given that Mark has presented this ritual as being âfor the remission of sins.â69 There are several different ways to explain why Jesus would have sought out this baptism:

Jesus had sinned and therefore sought repentance. While this idea does not cohere with Latter-day Saint theology, it is not explicitly denied in the text and is perhaps what some of Markâs audience would have assumed.

Jesus, while sinless himself, was identifying himself with sinners (compare Isa. 53:12) and taking their sins upon him. Therefore, he required baptism. This reading coheres with the presentation of masses from the south converging on John for baptism while one figure from the north meets John for baptism: Jesus is standing in for âallâ people. Jesusâ baptism would prefigure the Atonement, where Jesus took upon him other peopleâs sins. This view is supported by the fact that in the only other reference that Jesus makes to baptism in Markâs Gospel,70Jesus uses âbaptismâ as a metaphor for his death. Perhaps Jesus understood his baptism as, in some sense, foreshadowing his own death or preparatory for his death.

Modern Christian (including Latter-day Saint) theologies often interpret Jesusâ baptism as showing an example or pattern for everyone else to follow. However, it does not appear to be used this way in Markâs Gospel.

Immediately following Jesusâ baptism in this text, he has a visionary experience. Because these events are linked in the text, the baptism was in some sense a preparation for the vision.

Jesus, while sinless, did not realize that he was sinless and therefore sought out baptism.

Jesus was baptized to identify himself with Johnâs ministry and as a follower of John, despite the fact that Jesus did not have sins that required remittance. Jesus is willing to associate himself with self-professed sinners, even to be mistaken for one of them, in his act of baptism; this is an expression of his humility.

Later in this verse, when Jesus is baptized âin Jordan,â a different preposition is used than when the masses were baptized âin Jordan.â71This may be a meaningless distinction, but it might imply that there is some sense in which Jesusâ baptism is different from the other baptisms that John performed (as the heavenly vision that follows it indicates). If this is the case, then the text may be hinting at Jesusâ sinlessness through the different nature of his baptism.

Mark seems to delight in violating the audienceâs expectations. Surely the HB prophecies and Johnâs talk of a coming one who would baptize in the spirit led them to expect a strong figure. To see Jesus then appear as a lone man from an out-of-the-way place and seek baptism is a profound inversion of the audienceâs expectations. Perhaps the point of Jesusâ baptism is to emphasize his humility; the juxtaposition of Jesusâ humility and his regal status will be a theme throughout Markâs Gospel.

The ways in which Jesusâ baptism and the massesâ baptisms are contrasted are many (all/one, south/north, Jerusalem/Nazareth), and climax when, at the moment when the audience would have expected Jesus to confess his sins, he instead has a vision. The vision therefore can be taken as a confirmation of his sinlessness: presumably the approving voice from heaven would not have come had Jesus had sins that needed confessing. If this is the best reading, it confirms the fact that Mark often teaches subtly what the other Gospel writers teach explicitly and plainly.

The baptism is preceded by Johnâs prophecy that Jesus will baptize with the Spirit and followed by the Spiritâs descent upon Jesus from heaven; it is possible to see the baptism as the ritual that made the receipt of the Spirit possible for Jesus and thus commissioned him. Jesus performs no miracles, calls no disciples, and engages in no ministry before his baptismal experience. So while Jesusâ baptism did not remit any sins, it was a necessary part of his preparation.

The focus should not be on the baptism per se but on the visionary experience that immediately followed it and was similar to the prophetic call stories found in Isaiah 6, Jeremiah 1, and Ezekiel 1â2.

Some ancient Christian thinkers argued that Jesus âneeded no purifying rites himselfâhis purpose was to hallow water.â72While that may initially sound excessively apologetic or poetic, a similar dynamic does exist in the stories where Jesusâ touching a leper or an unclean woman does not have the expected effect of making him unclean but rather of cleansing the other person (1:42; 5:30). This reading also aligns with the viewpoint that Jesusâ baptism is part of a âreverse exodus,â and so his immersion in the water has a miraculous effect similar to the parting of the Red Sea and therefore consecrates the water.

Mark did not feel that this issue needed clarification; whether that was because the solution was obvious or because it was unimportant is not knowable. While it is difficult to determine which reading is best,73 one thing is certain: there is a broad consensus that Jesusâ baptism is one of the most historically likely events in Jesusâ life because no one would have concocted a story where Jesus was baptized âfor a remission of sins.â The idea that the vision stands in for the confession of sin (option 9 above) has much to recommend it.

The idea that a ritual immersion would not be self-administered appears to have been an innovationâat least in this time and placeâon Johnâs part. The fact that Jesus sought out John to baptize him (instead of Jesus baptizing himself) indicates that Jesus approves of Johnâs innovation and affirms the idea that baptism required someone to perform it. Once again, Latter-day Saint notions of authority and ordinances are not directly taught in the text, but their underpinnings are supported.

1:10 And straightway coming up out of the water: This verse contains the first occurrence of âstraightway,â a word used with incredible frequency in Mark. There are three theories offered for the frequent use of this word:

It creates a sense of things happening very quickly and gives a rushing sense to the narrative. This is the most likely reason for the wordâs frequent use.

It is simply a way to move along the narrative (and so perhaps should be translated as âthenâ or ânextâ).

Because it was âused in the LXX to [translate] the common HB formula âwehinnehâ (ââand beholdââ),â74Mark uses it to make the text sound scriptural.

Here âstraightwayâ modifies âthen the heavens opened,â not âcoming up out of the waterâ so it closely links the baptism to the vision.

âComing up out of the waterâ presumes that Jesus had previously gone down into the water, so baptism by immersion is pictured here.

he saw the heavens opened: It is possible that the antecedent of âheâ is John, but it is more likely that it is Jesus since Jesus was the subject of 1:9 and will be addressed in 1:11.

The wording âhe sawâ implies that only Jesus saw the heavens open and the dove descend. Of course, the audience of Markâs Gospel is also privy to this information; this is one of many times that the audience enjoys âinsiderâ status in this Gospel.

âThe heavensâ could be translated as âsky,â which may be preferable so as not to give the impression that heaven was destroyed as it was torn open. It is likely that the heavens/sky here was understood to be the firmament (Gen. 1:6â8) that separated humans from heaven, so its tearing open would then be symbolic of increased access between humans and God. Because the firmament is part of the created order in Genesis 1, tearing the firmament may feed the new creation theme in this chapter.

âOpenedâ is too tame a translation for the Greek word schizÅ,75 which means âtear,â ârip,â or âshred,â with âthe idea of violence . . . present in the verb.â76

and the Spirit like a dove descending upon him: âHolyâ does not modify Spirit here; Mark may be simply abbreviating since there was just a reference to the Holy Spirit. In the HB, the Spirit has the following functions:

making prophesying possible (Num. 11:25; 1 Sam. 10:6, 10)

providing physical strength (Judg. 14:6, 19)

physically transporting someone to a different location (1 Kgs. 18:12; 2 Kgs. 2:16; Ezek. 3:12, 14)

providing leadership ability (Judg. 3:10)

providing courage (Micah 3:8)

providing wisdom (Isa. 11:2)

The most relevant reference for understanding the role of the Spirit here is Isaiah 61:1â2, where the Spirit from God endows the chosen leader with the ability to perform a task.

This bestowal of the Spirit upon Jesus might be understood as a prerequisite to Jesusâ own ability to baptize in the Spirit, as John has just mentioned.

There are several possibilities for what âlike a doveâ would have conveyed to Markâs early audiences. The first consideration is: What does the phrase âlike a doveâ describe?

It describes the appearance of the Spirit. Given that Jesus sees the Spirit descend, the Spirit had a physical manifestation and so looked like a dove. Some Restoration scriptures seem to support this view, indicating that the Holy Ghost descended in the âformâ of a dove (1 Ne. 11:27; 2 Ne. 31:8; D&C 93:15).

It describes the manner of the Spiritâs descent, which leads to the question: What does it mean to descend like a dove? Does it suggest gentleness, slowness, rapidity, precision, or something else?77

Joseph Smith taught that, from before the Creation, the dove was a âsignâ for the Holy Spirit,78 leading some Latter-day Saint thinkers to conclude that âthe dove was not literally [presumably: physically?] present.â79 Possible symbolic resonances for the dove include the following:

In Genesis 1:2, the Spirit âhoversâ (KJV: âmovedâ) over the waters, using a verb that can describe the motion of a bird (see Deut. 32:11). While the verb used in LXX Genesis 1:2 does not have the connotation of a hovering bird, in later Jewish tradition the âhoveringâ would be identified with a dove.80If this image is in Markâs mind, then Jesusâ baptism is associated with the Creation (with links also to be found in the references to water and the heavens), and Jesusâ baptism might even be understood as a new creation.

After the flood, the dove serves as a sign to Noah that the crisis is over (Gen. 8:11). In this reading, Jesusâ body is paralleled with Noahâs ark, implying that it is a vehicle for salvation for those who choose to join with him.

In Greek mythology and the Greek tradition generally, the entrance of a bird into a human signified that the person had divine status or was a god in human form. So âmembers of the audience familiar with Greek mythology would understand v. 10 to mean that the earthly Jesus, from the time of his baptism, was a divine being walking the earth.â81

In Matthew 10:16 the dove suggests innocence, so its use in Mark would be an indirect way of emphasizing Jesusâ sinlessness at precisely the moment in the narrative where the audience had been led to expect Jesus to confess his sins.

The dove was sometimes a symbol for Israel. If that association was already present in Markâs time, then Jesus is viewed as a symbol for Israel, an idea that nicely aligns with the metaphor of Jesus as a New Israel or new creation. It also fits with the reverse Exodus theme.

The dove could be used in the HB sacrifices (Lev. 1:14), so perhaps its presence at Jesusâ baptism is meant to emphasize the idea of holiness and sanctification.

Given that Johnâs baptismal practice is innovative, perhaps the dove is as well, and efforts to find a precise analogue to explain its meaning are fruitless.

Because this detail is found in all four Gospelsâwhich is rareâit is possible that it was part of a widespread, well-known tradition that Mark felt obligated to include despite not having any interest in developing the imagery; this would explain why it is so enigmatic.

After the baptism, Jesus was ascending (Greek: anabainÅ) from the water; here the dove is descending (Greek: katabainÅ; this word is similar to âascendingâ). This inversion heightens the sense that the bestowal of the Spirit is one of the results of Jesusâ choice to be baptized.

Some manuscripts read âto/into him,â and others read âupon himâ here.82 It is likely that âto/intoâ was the earlier reading, with the change to âuponâ83 made either to parallel the other Gospels (Matt. 3:16; Luke 3:22; John 1:32) or because it was considered important to the theological debates about the nature of Jesusâ identity that were raging when the text was copied to have the Spirit rest âuponâ him instead of âinâ him.84

HB Allusions. The idea of the heavens being ripped apart may allude to Isaiah 64:1, where the prophet prays for the ripping open of the heavens and a victory for the Lord. Similarly, in Ezekiel 1:1, the heavens open immediately before a prophet receives a vision calling him to a certain task. These associations help the audience understand that the opening is not so much novel or destructive as it is an allusive manner of suggesting that the Lord is present in a new and real way and is acting in history.

Jesusâ Baptism and Death Compared. The only other use of the verb translated as âopenedâ (Greek: schizÅ) in Markâs Gospel is in 15:38, where immediately after Jesusâ death the temple veil is ripped open from top to bottom. There are about a half dozen other verbal and thematic similarities between these two stories, suggesting that Mark wants the audience to interpret Jesusâ death in light of his baptism. (See the Notes on 15:38 for a fuller discussion of these parallels and their implications.)

1:11 And there came a voice from heaven, saying: âCameâ is the same verb used in âcame to passâ in 1:9.

Some ancient manuscripts read âand a voice was heard from the heavens,â85 although this is less likely to be original. Some ancient manuscripts omit âand there cameâ (either accidentally or to harmonize with Matt. 3:17),86 leaving âand a voice from the heavens: Thou art . . .â87 The variant preferred here does not seem to impact the interpretation of the passage, but the shortest reading does have a certain dramatic appeal and perhaps underscores the suddenness of the event. As the italics in the KJV indicate, the word âsayingâ was not part of the Greek text from which they were translating but was added so the verse would read more smoothly in English.

Thou art my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased: As a description of Jesus that comes straight from heaven, this sentence is extremely important. The word âyouâ is given emphasis in Greek. There are two significant translation options here:

âBelovedâ could be translated as âonly,â which might make sense in this context, given that Mark sets up a contrast between the masses who come to John from the south for baptism and the single person, Jesus, who comes from the north. It would also emphasize the idea that Jesus has a unique relationship with God.

âBeloved/onlyâ could be translated as a title: âYou are my son, the Beloved Oneâ or âYou are my son, the Only One.â

There are two different ways that the relationship between the two phrases (âThou art . . .â and âin whom . . .â) can be understood:

Regardless of whether it is translated as âbelovedâ or âonly,â that phrase speaks to Jesus himself, while âin whom I am well pleasedâ speaks to Jesusâ choices and actions. In other words, Jesus is loved just for being who he is but also brings pleasure to God because of his actions. There are some interesting theological implications here for how the audience understands Godâs love and pleasure.

Perhaps âin whom I am well pleasedâ should have a âthereforeâ before it, which would mean that God is pleased because Jesus is his son, not because of any particular actions that Jesus has taken.

See also the Notes on 9:7, where a voice from heaven will make a very similar pronouncement.

Relationship to Psalm 2, Isaiah 42, and Genesis 22. The statement from heaven is a combination of two (or three) texts:

LXX Psalm 2:7 (KJV: âThou art my sonâ) but with three changes:

Mark omits âthis day have I begotten thee.â There are two different ways to understand this omission:

It was left out because that phrase was not relevant to Jesusâ situation.88

It was left out, but its presence should be assumed by the audience, so there is some sense in which Jesus was âbegottenâ at his baptism.

Psalm 2:7 doesnât include the word âbeloved/only.â Perhaps âbeloved/onlyâ is imported from the story of the near-sacrifice of Isaac (LXX Gen. 22:2, 12, 16); this would be the third HB text quoted here. If so, it is an âominous noteâ89that foreshadows Jesusâ sacrificial death by associating it with Isaacâs story.

In Psalm 2:7, in both Hebrew and Greek, there is no definite article and âmy sonâ is the first phrase, meaning that âa son of mine [are] youâ is a reasonable translation. But âMark puts . . . âyouâ in first position to accent the identification of Jesus as Godâs Son.â90This change emphasizes Jesusâ relationship to God.

The material in Psalm 2 undergirds what the voice from heaven is teaching about Jesus and his identity and mission: this psalm is considered a royal enthronement psalm, meaning that its setting is the coronation of a new king, and it may have been used at royal coronations.91But not only is it about the crowning of a new king, it is also about the identity of the king. Psalm 2:6 reads, âYet have I set my king upon my holy hill of Zion,â and Psalm 2:8â9 promises this son an inheritance of all that the Lord has and great power. Further, it makes a contrast between earthly rulers and the chosen ruler described in the psalm; this distinction, if behind the reference in Mark, suggests that Jesus is different from earthly kings. To members of Markâs audience familiar with this psalm, the use of a line from it by the voice from heaven would have emphasized Jesusâ unique relationship and powers.

Isaiah 42:1 describes the servant âin whom my soul delighteth.â Isaiah 42:1â7 is an important text in terms of understanding Jesusâ mission: it points to Jesusâ care for those on the margins of society (Isa. 42:3, 7), his role as mediator of the covenant (Isa. 42:4), and his eventual success despite obstacles (Isa. 42:4). The reference to âspiritâ (Isa. 42:1) is another link to the present context. As one scholar explained, âThe servant of Yahweh portrayed in Isaiah 42:1â4 is a nonviolent figure who achieves justice for the nations by patient faithfulness, under the direction of the Spirit of Yahweh.â92

The reference to the âonly/belovedâ may come from Genesis 22, where it is used repeatedly to describe Isaac. This may be a hint of Jesusâ eventual death.

Relationship to Isaiah 61:1â3. While not directly quoted by the voice from heaven, Isaiah 61:1â3 may be alluded to, as there are several points of connection between that text and chapter 1:

The role of the Spirit, which comes upon Jesus in Mark and is explained in the Isaiah passage.

Isaiah 61:1 refers to âgood tidings,â using a form of the Greek word translated as âgospelâ in 1:1.

Isaiah 61:1 refers to the anointed one, using a form of the Greek word translated as âChristâ in 1:1.

The healing and release of captives in Isaiah may be understood as a way of describing the exorcism that will happen near the beginning of Jesusâ ministry (1:23).

Isaiahâs âyear of the Lordâ (Isa. 61:2) is conceptually similar to the summary statement of Jesusâ preaching: âthe time is fulfilledâ (1:15).

If Mark is encouraging the audience to associate that text from Isaiah with the inauguration of Jesusâ ministry, then there is encouragement to see Jesusâ mission as a fulfillment of HB prophecy that is focused on blessing those on the margins of society and preaching a message of hope.

1:12 And immediately the Spirit driveth him into the wilderness: Markâs use of âimmediatelyâ reinforces the fast-paced nature of the narrative. It suggests an association between this passage and Jesusâ baptism: being driven into the wilderness is a direct result of being baptized.

The audience would have expected the Spirit to give Jesus power to perform some mighty act or deliver some impressive teaching, so it is most surprising that the first action of the Spirit, after descending upon Jesus at his baptism, is to drive Jesus into the wilderness. It is not clear whether the purpose of the Spirit sending Jesus into the wilderness was so Jesus could be tempted, or if there was some other purpose and then the temptation followed. It is possible that, much as God tested Israel in the wilderness, Godâs spirit will here test Jesus in the wilderness.

There are three references to the Spirit in this first section of the Gospel of Mark (1:8, 10, 12), but only three more throughout the entire rest of the text (3:29; 12:36; 13:11).93 It may be that much as âthe Wordâ is a key concept in the beginning of the Gospel of John, the Spirit is a key concept in the beginning of the Gospel of Mark. In the HB, the presence of the Spirit was associated with the end of days (for example, Isa. 32:15; Ezek. 36:26â27; and Joel 2:28â29). So perhaps the cluster of references to the Spirit at the beginning of Markâs Gospel is meant to emphasize the idea that the beginning of the end was near.

âDrivethâ is in the historical present tense,94 which means that events in the past are narrated in the present tense. This form might have been particularly appropriate to an oral presentation as it draws the listener in by portraying the story as if it were happening in the current moment.

The word translated as âdrivethâ can connote âmoral compulsion,â95 force, and, sometimes, violence; this is the same word used by Mark to describe demons being cast out (for example, in 1:34, 39). It is surprising to imagine Jesus being forced by the Spirit; perhaps the point is that Jesus does not resist the Spirit. In the HB, a common function of the Spirit is to move a person from place to place (see the Notes on 1:10), which suggests that the action is not as unusual as it might strike the modern reader.

Soon, Mark will introduce a man controlled by an âunclean spiritâ (1:23); perhaps this story presents the opposite, where Jesus here is under the control of a Holy Spirit. While Latter-day Saints donât speak of Jesus being âpossessedâ by the Holy Spirit, the idea that Jesus submitted to Godâs will in all things is well understood, and, while the language here is different, the concept is the same.

On a literal level, âinto the wildernessâ is confusing since Jesus was just baptized by John âin the wilderness.â But Markâs purpose here is more theological than geographical: the point is that Jesus will have a âwildernessâ experience as he is tempted. The wilderness can typify the dwelling place of chaos and evil; others have understood it as the place where God is most likely to visit (for example, Isa. 32:14â20); in this passage, both views could be harmonized with the idea that God visits the people after a time of testing.

1:13 And he was there in the wilderness forty days: Because âin the wildernessâ was used at the end of the previous verse, Mark is either redundant or deliberate. The latter is more likely; Mark is emphasizing Jesusâ wilderness location because of its metaphorical significance.

In the HB, numbers are sometimes used symbolically, and forty is often a symbol for trial, testing, or learning.96 While Mark does not specifically mention that Jesus fasted in the wilderness, the times of testing in the HB associated with the number forty normally include references to fasting and/or food, so it is possible that Mark assumed it.97

tempted of Satan: âTemptedâ can have a full spectrum of nuances:

Positive(âdetermine oneâs mettleâ). Focusing on the Spiritâs role in delivering Jesus into the wilderness implies that a positive meaning was intended.

Neutral(âtestâ). Given that both the Spirit and Satan have a role in Jesusâ tempting, a neutral reading might be appropriate,98especially since this verb in its other uses in Markâs Gospel99involves (mortal) opponents of Jesus where âtestâ is probably a better translation than âtempt.â

Negative(âtemptâ). Focusing on Satanâs role suggests a negative meaning, with Satan encouraging Jesus to violate Godâs commandments.

While Matthew and Luke mention three temptations, Mark does not give a number; it is possible that more than three temptations are implied here. Many readers assume that, after this passage, Jesusâ mortal temptations ended since they are not mentioned again (save for, as mentioned above, the human opponents who test Jesus), but there is no reason to assume that Jesus was not tempted throughout his entire mortal life. It is possible to read Jesusâ mortal ministry as bookended by temptationsâin the wilderness and in Gethsemane. This suggests that Jesusâ life was literally surrounded by temptation and suffering.

and was with the wild beasts: âWild beastsâ has a negative connotation in English that is absent from the Greek text, which might be translated as, simply, âanimals.â But still their role is not clear.100 Options include:

In Greek thought, wild beasts were associated with demons.101In this reading, the wild beasts would be part of the temptations.

In the HB, the absence of wild beasts is often linked with Godâs protection,102and the presence of wild animals is sometimes associated with Godâs judgment or punishment (for example, Num. 21:6; Isa. 34:14), so the presence of the beasts here could either

indicate that God is not protecting Jesus during this experience. This could be particularly relevant to Markâs audience, whose persecution might have included being literally thrown to the lions.

indicate that, because the beasts do not harm Jesus, God is protecting Jesus during this experience.

The beasts might allude to the story of Daniel in the lionsâ den (Dan. 6:10â28), with the expectation that the animals would harm Jesus, but divine protection prohibited that outcome. In this reading, the animals become a symbol of Jesusâ ability to overcome challenges and the divine protection that he enjoys.

The animals could represent the ânaturalâ dangers of the fallen world, which Jesus must experience along with the supernatural temptations represented by Satan. Thus, both physical and spiritual forces test his resolve to be faithful to God.

Wild beasts are often presented in the HB as evidence of desolation (for example, Deut. 8:15; Isa. 13:21â22; 34:11), so here they would contribute to the âwildernessâ theme or magnify Jesusâ temptations.

In Danielâs vision (Dan. 7:3â7) and in the book of Revelation, animals are symbols for political entities; perhaps there is a subtle nod to the political themes that will be more prominent later in Markâs Gospel.

The animals are part of the post-temptation scene showing that Jesus has overcome the temptations: perhaps the beasts, like the angels, are serving Jesus.

In Isaiah 43:19â21, God promises to do something new: after making a way in the wilderness (compare to 1:2â3), the righteous and animals will be able to live in peace. In this view, the animals are part of the post-temptation scene and fulfill HB prophecy (see Isa. 11:6â9; 65:25; and Hosea 2:18).

TheTestament of Naphtali,an apocryphal book, refers to the devil, wild beasts, and angels and describes the wild beasts as âafraidâ of the righteous one. This is a compelling parallel to 1:13, but it may have been written (or edited) under the influence of the accounts of Jesusâ temptations, which would negate its value as an independent witness to the symbolic meaning of the wild beasts in this story.

Most of these theories fall into two groups, based on whether the presence of the wild beasts is negative (part of the temptation) or positive (evidence of overcoming the temptation). The inability to determine which is correct is largely due to the inability to determine whether the animals are present during or after the temptation.

and the angels ministered unto him: âAngelsâ is the same word used in 1:2 (KJV: âmessengerâ), so it is possible to read this incident as (part of) the fulfillment of that scriptural promise.

It is not clear what âministeredâ means in this passage, partially because under the influence of Christian thought and practice, the meaning of this word transitioned. The two main options are:

Physical, often menial, service, such as serving food. Perhaps the angels minister to Jesus by protecting him from the animals or providing him with food or otherwise attending to his physical needs.

Spiritual, in the modern sense of being a minister, such as providing spiritual aid, counsel, and/or comfort.

The ministering of the angels can be understood to have several different rhetorical functions:

It connotes that the angels are subservient to Jesus, perhaps specifically because of his success in overcoming temptation.

The need for Jesus to be aided by others shows the limits that mortality places upon him.

The fact that John the Baptist had to procure his own food (locusts and wild honey)âbut Jesus does notâillustrates Johnâs teaching that Jesus would be âmightierâ than he was.

In 1 Kings 19:4â8, an angelic messenger provides Elijah with food that strengthens him for his journey. If Mark had that text in mind, perhaps these angels are, similarly, feeding Jesus to equip him for the opening phase of his ministry. Further, Jesus is associated with Elijah as a prophet.

If Jesus is understood as âthe new Israel,â then the angels are providing his âmannaâ during his time in the wilderness.

Relationship to Genesis. The introductory section of Mark has several possible echoes of Genesis 1â3, including:

Genesis 1:1 starts with âin the beginning,â which in the LXX uses the same word for âbeginningâ (Greek:archÄ) as 1:1.103Perhaps Mark alluded to that text in order to suggest that Jesusâ story was as much a new beginning for humankind as the creation of the world.

When Mark mentions the voice crying in the wilderness, the idea of a âvoiceâ calling a new situation into being may allude to the voice of God causing the Creation in Genesis 1. If this allusion was intentional, then it serves to suggest that Jesusâ ministry offers a new beginning for the world.

The voice from heaven pronouncing Jesus a beloved son might be paralleled with Godâs voice in Genesis 1, announcing that each phase of the Creation is good. This would align with other elements of new creation imagery in Mark 1, including the âhoveringâ dove, the opened firmament, and the water. If Jesus is the ânew creation,â then this might explain and account for his sinlessness.

When the Spirit drives Jesus into the wilderness after his baptism, the word âdrivethâ (Greek:ekballÅ) is the same word used in LXX Genesis 3:24 when Adam and Eve are removed from the garden after the Fall. There may be a useful contrast to be made between Jesus and Adam:

If Mark had the story of the Fall in mind, then history is running backwards, as Jesus is driven out, then tested, then has an experience with animals. (By contrast, in Genesis, Adam names the animals, is tested, and is then driven out.)

The fact that Jesus did not merit his driving out through disobedience may hint at Markâs Atonement theology here, as Jesus is suffering a punishment that he did not deserve.

If Jesus is the new Adam, then the angelsâ service is an inversion of the role of the cherubim, who keep Adam from the tree of life: Jesusâ angels provide food while Adamâs cherubim kept him away from food. This difference emphasizes Jesusâ sinlessness.

Animals are hostile to humans after the Fall; the fact that Jesus is here shown at peace with animals implies that a new paradisaical state has been created, perhaps specifically as a result of his ability to withstand temptation.

The overall effect of these echoes of Genesis 1â3 in chapter 1 is to imply that a new creation is occurring in the ministry of Jesus.

Relationship to Elijah. Like Jesus in this story, Elijah spends time in a wilderness area, is associated with the number forty, and is helped by angels (1 Kgs. 19:4â8). But in that story, Elijah is very discouraged; perhaps there is a contrast with Jesus, who does not seem to be discouraged by his difficult circumstances.

The prologue featured comparisons between John the Baptist and Elijah, but the association here between Jesus and Elijah may be designed to help the audience recognize that John was not the only representation of Elijah; Jesus had some things in common with Elijah as well.

Relationship to Job. As in the story of Job, where the adversary tests a righteous man, Jesus is tested in Markâs story. Both temptations come immediately after God has praised the faithfulness of the man (1:11; Job 1:8). The difference, however, is that in Job, the real dispute is between God and Satan, but in Markâs story, the dispute is between Jesus and Satan, which suggests by analogy Jesusâ divine status.104

Relationship to Psalm 91. Mark seems to be alluding to Psalm 91,105 with its references to the support of angels (Ps. 91:11) and the control of threatening animals (Ps. 91:13). The larger context of that psalm is one of trust and faith in God by the psalmist, so an allusion would emphasize that Jesus was able to be victorious in the wilderness because he trusted God to deliver him from all threats.

In Jewish tradition, Psalm 91 was sometimes associated with exorcism and protection from evil spirits,106 and so this psalm might be a bridge from the baptism to the exorcism in 1:21â28. It also emphasizes that Jesusâ success in fighting off temptation is related to his understanding of its evil root.

Psalm 91:14 (âBecause he hath set his love upon me, therefore will I deliver him: I will set him on high, because he hath known my nameâ) is particularly significant as a recap of the baptismal scene, emphasizing Godâs love for and acknowledgment of Jesusâ special status. Recognizing the allusions to Psalm 91 allows the audience to see that Mark is using this scene as a commentary on Jesusâ relationship with God.

Relationship to Isaiahâs Prophecy. Isaiah 35:8â10 speaks of a âhighwayâ (compare the references in the prologue) that the righteous will travel where no beast will be present and where the redeemed and ransomed will return in the Lord in a future time. There are plenty of echoes between this passage in Isaiah and this section in Markâs Gospel; they imply that the time of which Isaiah had prophesied has come.

The following themes from Isaiah can be identified in chapter 1 of Mark:

wilderness

proclaiming forgiveness

tearing of the heavens

descent of the Spirit

heavenly voice

animals

âgood newsâ

These associations, as well as the (possibly inaccurate) reference to Isaiah in 1:2, may suggest that Mark deliberately structured this section of the Gospel to evoke Isaiahâs message.

1:14 Now after that John was put in prison: Some of the ancient manuscripts have âbutâ (Greek: de) instead of âandâ (Greek: kai; translated as ânowâ in the KJV).107 The âbutâ heightens the contrast between 1:13 and this verse; perhaps those who arrested John are foils for the angels who served Jesus in the wilderness.

This verse does not use the standard verb for âputâ but rather a verb sometimes translated as âdelivered upâ (Greek: paradidÅmai); it emphasizes the parallels between Johnâs arrest and Jesusâ arrest later, where the same verb is used.108 Perhaps part of Johnâs task to prepare the way for the Lord (or Johnâs role as teacher for Jesus, if indeed Jesus was Johnâs disciple) was to show Jesus the way by being âdelivered upâ first. Jesus makes the comparison between Johnâs experience and his own experience explicit in 9:12â13. The reference to John being put in prison is a bit of foreshadowing: if Jesus is Johnâs follower and John goes to prison, then Jesusâ fate will be similar.

This verse implies that John and Jesus did not have active ministries at the same time. (It is also possible to understand this verse as saying that Jesus did preach before John was arrested, just not in Galilee.109) It seems that Markâs concern was âmore theological than chronological.â110 One of the themes of 2:1â3:6 is the incompatibility of the âoldâ and the ânew.â Perhaps as a result of this teaching, Mark thought it would not do to have John and Jesus preaching at the same time111 in light of the idea that John had preached that a âmightier oneâ was coming; the lack of a concurrent ministry emphasizes Johnâs role as someone who prepared the way for Jesus. Once the preparation is made, there is a theological necessity in Mark for John to step off of the stage. Mark will say more about John in chapter 6ânot because it is chronologically appropriate there, but rather because it allows Mark to make a contrast between Johnâs disciples and Jesusâ disciples.

Jesus came into Galilee: John went out into the wilderness, but Jesus preaches in areas where people live. Perhaps âthe geographical contrast [between the wilderness and Galilee] suggests a thematic and qualitative distinction between the time of repentance and asceticism in the wilderness and the time of good news, fulfillment and trust in Galilee.â112

preaching the gospel of the kingdom of God: Some ancient manuscripts read âthe gospel of Godâ instead of âthe gospel of the kingdom of Godâ here.113 The phrase âthe kingdom ofâ was probably added by scribes because:

It makes the summary of Jesusâ teachings in this verse align more closely with the report of Jesusâ teachings in the next verse.

The phrase âthe kingdom of Godâ is more common in the NT. âThe gospel of Godâ is much less common (it is not otherwise used in Markâs Gospel and is used only a few times in the rest of the NT), perhaps making it sound odd to the copyists.

There are two ways to interpret âof Godâ in this verse:

âThe good news about Godâ

âThe good news that God deliversâ

This is the same word (Greek: kÄryssÅ) used in 1:4 to describe Johnâs preaching, so it emphasizes the continuity between the two of them. Yet there is a distinction in that John preached about the coming one, and Jesus preaches about the kingdom of God.

This verse can be viewed as an important moment of transition within the text, with everything before it giving a divine perspective on who Jesus is (something no one in the story has), while this verse shifts to the perspective of a mortal and what she or he would have been able to observe of Jesusâ ministry.

1:15 And saying, The time is fulfilled: The idea here is that a certain period of time has reached its end; the perfect verb tense indicates that the time has already been fulfilled. This is presumably a divine passive, indicating that God is the understood subject and thus the one who has fulfilled the time. In other words, this time was prophesied about and now is here. The idea of time being fulfilled may come from Daniel 7:22, which prophesies of a coming time when âthe saints [will possess] the kingdom.â By making a statement such as this, Jesus is acting like a prophet who functions as a herald, bringing the news (see, for example, Isa. 56:1).

and the kingdom of God is at hand: There is great debate114 as to the better interpretation of âat handâ in this verse:

âAt handâ means âhereâ (âthe kingdom of God is hereâ). Support for this reading comes from

2:21â22, which indicates that the âoldâ and the ânewâ cannot be mixed.

the fact that John the Baptist was in prison before Jesus began to preach, suggesting a break with the old order of things.

âAt handâ means ânearâ (âthe kingdom of God is nearâ). Mark 13:28 lends support to the idea of the kingdom being near but not yet present. Further, it is possible to view âthe kingdom of Godâ as coming in a special sense with the mortal life of Jesus Christ and/or his Atonement and Resurrection.

âAt handâ is ambiguous, possibly encompassing both âhereâ and ânear.â

Which of these options the reader finds most persuasive depends to some extent on how âthe kingdom of Godâ is defined:

The Davidic kingdom

The spiritual reign of God in the human heart

The end of history with final judgment

The rule of God now, but with more to come in the future (in other words, the beginning of the rule of God)

The obligation humans owe to God as subjects115

What is at stake here is whether Jesus is preaching that the kingdom of God had already arrived or was about to arrive. If he is saying that it was about to arrive, then the kingdom of God would, presumably, have reference to his Atonement and Resurrection and perhaps his establishment of a community of believers. If he is saying that it had arrived, then presumably the kingdom of God was tied primarily to his incarnation.

Because this verse is parallel to the previous verse, consideration of the parallel might help determine the meaning, but there are still two options:

The kingdom of God is âhere,â yielding synonymous parallelism with âhas been fulfilled.â

The kingdom of God is âat hand,â to provide a heightening of the parallelism with âhas been fulfilled.â

In either case, âat handâ may be related to the repetition of âimmediatelyâ previously in the chapter as a way to emphasize the closeness of the appointed time. (See also the Notes on 9:1, which contains a similar teaching.)

repent ye, and believe the gospel: âRepentâ is plural, as is âbelieveâ below. âRepentâ carried the implication of change, as in changing oneâs mind; it might be more similar to the way modern Latter-day Saints use the term âconvertâ and could be understood as a call to turn away from the previous way of life. In an HB context, similar language usually implies âreturning [to God].â116

This reference to the gospel may form a bookend with the reference in 1:1, framing 1:1â15 as the prologue or âbeginningâ of the gospel. Here, âbelieveâ probably has the connotation of trust rather than of offering intellectual assent to a proposition. Therefore, it âdenotes not only an intellectual acceptance that the ânewsâ is true, but a response of acceptance and commitment.â117 So it is possible to translate this verse, which contains a summary of the core of Jesusâ preaching mission, as a call to

change your mind and trust the good news.

convert and trust the good news.

convert and believe in the gospel.

Regardless of the preferred translation here, there is a compelling parallel in this short statement of Jesusâ teachings, with two perfect tense indicative verbs (the time âhad been fulfilledâ and the kingdom of God âhas come nearâ) balanced by two imperatives (ârepentâ and âbelieveâ). âRepentâ could encapsulate oneâs relationship to the past, and âbelieveâ would express oneâs relationship to the future. The two declarations lead to two actions, suggesting a tight link between action and response.

Analysis

After hearing the HB prophecies and Johnâs multiple declarations about the superiority of the âcoming one,â the audience is primed for the arrival of an extremely important person. Through his preaching about the coming mightier oneâone so important that John is unworthy to be his slave, let alone his discipleâJohn creates the expectation of the arrival of a truly spectacular person. Mark then toys with the audienceâs expectations by presenting a man with no pedigree, coming from the least important place imaginable, seeking baptism âfor the remission of sins.â Mark thus teaches that Jesus will simultaneously conform to and violate expectations of what the Coming One should be. In another instance of violated expectations, the biblical quotes that Mark has placed in the introduction have the Coming One going to Jerusalem (Isa. 40:1â5) and the temple,118 but Mark introduces Jesus in the wilderness. So Jesus would not have seemed to be the âcoming oneâ that the audience was led to expect. But then the voice from heaven, placed in the narrative precisely where the audience had been led to expect a confession of sins, affirms that God is pleased with Jesus. Mark has begun to teach what will become a theme in this Gospel: Jesus is the Coming One, but he is likely not what has been expectedâhe simultaneously meets and denies expectations. Markâs styleâcharacterized by subtlety and allusionâis on full display here.

Jesusâ baptism is narrated in a minimalistic way, and the emphasis of the passage is really on the postbaptismal experience of the Spirit and the voice from heaven; Mark seems more concerned with the latter event. Several aspects of Jesusâ baptism suggest that Jesus fulfills the role of the âNew Israel.â First, John baptized masses (âallâ) of people who came to him from the south; by contrast, Jesus is one person coming to John from the north. This suggests that Jesus in himself is somehow equated with those masses of people; Mark may intend to portray Jesus here as Israel personified. This association sets the stage for understanding Jesusâ ability to atone for the sins of all people.

The combination of quotations from Isaiah 42:1 and Psalm 2:7 in the statement made by the voice from heaven shapes the perception of Jesusâ identity. The royal setting and themes of the psalm present Jesus as someone who is regal and exalted, while the Isaiah text presents a servant of the Lord who must suffer. The hint of Genesis 22 points to Jesusâ death. But Genesis 22 concerns Abrahamâs commitment to God, while in Markâs context, the issue is Godâs commitment to humans;119 while subtle, this is an evocative way for Markâs audience to think about Jesusâ death. So the juxtaposition of these two texts conveys a paradoxical and crucial principle about Jesusâ ministry. This is a perfect introduction to Jesus as it presents him as the âparadox of a suffering, unrecognized Messiah.â120 The same paradox is found in the form of the text, as Jesus is introduced by both a very humble voice (Johnâs, saying that he is unworthy to be Jesusâ slave) and a mighty voice (Godâs, announcing pleasure with the beloved son).

Immediately after Jesus is baptized, he sees and hears something that apparently no one else in the story can. There are several different ways to understand what purpose this experience served:

Some early Christian thinkers read this story as Jesusâ âadoptionâ as Godâs son, implying that Jesus had no special status before this experience. This is not consonant with Latter-day Saint thought. However, Latter-day Saints understand that Jesus increased in knowledge, and it is possible that this experience was the first time he became aware of his special status.

While the idea that Jesus was Godâs beloved Son was not new to Jesus, this vision could have been a restatement or confirmation of it or a sign that it was time to begin his ministry.

Similar to prophetic call narratives (see Isa. 6; Jer. 1; Ezek. 1; 1 Ne. 1), this story is Jesusâ call to ministry.

The vision expresses Godâs approval of Jesusâ decision to be baptized.

For Markâs audience, the vision could have clarified that Jesus was not baptized âfor the remission of sinsâ because at precisely the point in the narrative where the audience would have expected him to confess his sins, he has an entirely different experience, one that includes divine approval for his actions.

Immediately after Jesus chooses to be baptized, the Spirit descends, and, immediately after that, Jesus is cast out into the wilderness where he is tempted. The repetition of the word âimmediatelyâ suggests a tight link between the decision to be baptized and Jesusâ wilderness temptations. Godâs pleasure with Jesus stems from Jesusâ willingness to be baptized, which is linked to his willingness to suffer temptations. (Later in Mark, in the only time that he speaks about baptism, Jesus will use baptism as a metaphor for his own death.) In this light, there is a parallel to the Atonement, where Jesusâ willingness to do what is right leads to his suffering. This parallel is particularly compelling if Jesus was baptized in behalf of all Israel. There is a sense in which this is a foreshadowing of the Atonement, where Jesus will take on the sins of all people. Perhaps the combination of spiritual testing (by Satan) and physical testing (by the presence of threatening animals and the wilderness setting) foreshadows the spiritual and physical aspects of the Atonement.

The prologue emphasizes âthe way of the Lordâ (1:3) and Johnâs role in preparing it; the role of the Spirit is also emphasized. Perhaps the last thing the audience would have expected would be that that âwayâ and that Spirit would lead Jesus to be tempted by Satan in the wilderness. It is a prime example of Markâs irony: the audience expects that John will prepare Jesusâ pathâthe path that Isaiah indicates is like the path of a glorious, conquering army (Isa. 40:3â4). Instead, Jesusâ path is shown not to be one of glory but one of temptation and even suffering. There is also irony in the compelling tension between continuity with Israelâs past (references to Isaiah and Elijah) and a radical departure from it (baptism for remission of sins instead of temple sacrifice and the invitation to âchange your life and believe the good newsâ).

Mark 1:1â8 focuss on John while 1:9â15 focus on Jesus. Both sections identify the person, locate him in the wilderness, note his preaching, and describe the preaching. Thus Jesus is âfollowingâ John (either in the sense of coming after him or in the sense of being his disciple), and the HB prophecies about âpreparing the wayâ are being fulfilled as Jesus acts in a manner similar to John. Yet there are significant differences as well, since the content of Jesusâ preaching is different: John preaches about the coming one and Jesus preaches about the kingdom of God.

Several parallels can be found between the experiences of Israel and Jesus (the wilderness, the Red Sea/baptism, temptation, caretaking angels), but in Jesusâ case, unlike in Israelâs case, there is no yielding to temptation. While the Gospel of Mark never explicitly teaches that Jesus is without sin, the presentation of Jesus as the ânew Israelâ who resists temptation would convey that idea in a subtle manner. With this comparison, there is a sense in which time is moving backwards: Johnâs baptism features people leaving the promised land to go into the wilderness, an inversion of the Exodus journey. They are baptized, which is perhaps analogous to the journey through the Red Sea. Continuing with the backwards march of time, Jesus is tempted and then surrounded by animals and angels. It is as if the fallen world has been reversed in Jesusâ story, which is a powerful background to Markâs Atonement theology.

Throughout the rest of Markâs Gospel, neither Jesusâ friends nor his adversaries are aware of his identity and his authority as it has been described here. But Markâs audience has heard the prologue, and this puts them in a different position from the people within the text. They already know who Jesus is, and they bring that knowledge to each story in the text as they observe the other characters discover (or refuse to discover) this knowledge for themselves.121

In the first fifteen verses, Mark has had HB prophets, a contemporary prophet (John the Baptist), a voice from heaven, Satan (indirectly), beasts (whether their purpose is helpful or harmful), and angels all attest to Jesusâ special status. This is quite the introduction to Jesus.

Jesus Calls Disciples(1:16â20)

King James Version

16 Now as he walked by the sea of Galilee, he saw Simon and Andrew his brother casting a net into the sea: for they were fishers.17 And Jesus said unto them, Come ye after me, and I will make you to become fishers of men.18 And straightway they forsook their nets, and followed him.19 And when he had gone a little further thence, he saw James the son of Zebedee, and John his brother, who also were in the ship mending their nets.20 And straightway he called them: and they left their father Zebedee in the ship with the hired servants, and went after him.

New Rendition

16 And walking beside the Sea of Galilee, he saw Simon and Andrewâthe brother of Simonâcasting a net in the sea, for they were fishers.17 And Jesus said to them, âCome follow me and I will make you into fishers of people.â18 And immediately, having left their nets, they followed him.19 And having gone on a little, he saw Jacob, Zebedeeâs son, and his brother John. And they were in a boat preparing the nets.20 And immediately he invited them. And having left their father Zebedee in the boat with the laborers, they followed him.

[/column2] [/column]

Notes

1:16 Now as he walked by the sea of Galilee: This sea is also called the Sea of Tiberias (John 6:1), the Sea of Gennesar, the Lake of Gennesaret (Luke 5:1), and the Sea of Chinnereth (Num. 34:11). As a freshwater, inland body, the best English term would be âlake,â but most translations use âseaâ since it is customary.

he saw Simon and Andrew his brother: The idea that Jesus saw Simon and Andrew may be a mundane note, or it may reflect something more: perhaps Jesus is âseeingâ (in a visionary sense) the future potential of these disciples.122 Because the only other previous reference in Mark to Jesus âseeingâ was the visionary experience immediately after his baptism, the audience is primed to find something more than normal sight here. Much as Jesus âsawâ his true identity as Godâs beloved son after his baptism, perhaps he here sees the true potential of these fishermen. If so, this brief phrase is an important component of Markâs picture of discipleship: the disciples frequently make mistakes, yet Jesus continues to patiently teach them, perhaps because of the vision of their potential that immediately preceded their call.

casting a net into the sea: for they were fishers: Some manuscripts lack âa netâ here.123

The net would have been circular, probably ten to fifteen feet in diameter, with stones (as weights) along the edges. There would have been a rope attached and it would have been pulled after the net was thrown into the water, trapping whatever fish were underneath. This type of fishing required a great deal of physical strength since it meant repeatedly throwing this large net weighed with stones either from a boat or while wading in the water.

The presence of the phrase âfor they were fishersâ is puzzling: who else would be casting a net in the water besides fishermen? Possible reasons for the inclusion of this phrase include the following:

Because the verb translated as âcasting a netâ is so unusual (in fact, this is its only use in the NT except for the parallel story in Matt. 4:18), Mark may have added this phrase to explain it to the audience. Of course, this raises the question of why Mark would use an unfamiliar verb and then explain it instead of using a more familiar verb.

Markâs audience, more urban than the Galilean setting of this story, required some clarification.

Mark wanted to introduce the word âfisherâ to set up the connection to the next verse, where the same word is used and likely has a symbolic dimension.

1:17 And Jesus said unto them, Come ye after me: âAfter meâ is a technical term meaning âbe my disciple.â

Standard practice was for students to choose which teacher to follow, so Jesus upsets expectations here by calling disciples: âJesusâ peremptory summons, with its expectation of radical renunciation even of family ties, goes far beyond anything they would be familiar with in normal society. It marks him as a prophet rather than a rabbi.â124 Jesus acts not like a rabbi or a philosopher but like the God of the HB, who calls Abraham (Gen. 12:1â4).

This calling is an important piece of the puzzle of discipleship in Mark: the disciples will make many mistakes as they learn how to follow Jesus; undergirding the portrayal of the disciples, however, is the knowledge that Jesus chose themâthey did not choose to follow him. Each instance of misunderstanding on the part of the disciples does not so much make them look inadequate but rather points to Jesusâ design in having chosen them to follow him.

Mark does not discuss what motivated them to follow Jesus; perhaps omitting the motivation speaks to Jesusâ authority to call them. (In other words, they followed him because he told them to, not because of their own assessment of Jesusâ worth as a teacher.) Perhaps Simon and Andrew had a prior association with Jesus, or perhaps this is the first time that they see him; the text permits either reading. Either scenario is powerful in its own way: in the first, it is time for them to give up their âday jobsâ for full-time discipleship, and they are willing and able to do this because of what they have seen of Jesus. In the latter scenario, there is something so compelling about Jesusâ brief words that they abandon all to follow him.

and I will make you to become fishers of men: The future tense âI will makeâ indicates that Jesus will make them into fishers in the future and perhaps envisions a period of training before that time.

Jesusâ language is reminiscent of the covenant formula in the HB as he promises to do something for the disciples if they honor their part of the contract. If the idea of being âfishers of menâ is primarily associated with acting as God does, then Jesus is offering to make the disciples godlike.

The perception of fishing as a hobby requiring great patience, little movement, quiet, and minimal physical effort would not apply here: the kind of fishing Peter and Andrew are engaged in required extensive and sustained effort. It was physically demanding, so the image of being âfishers of menâ should include this element of hard labor.

There are many possibilities for the symbolism of being fishers of men:125

In Greco-Roman tradition, a teacher is a âfisher,â which would emphasize the disciplesâ teaching role. It would also be ironic because Jesus is âfishingâ for disciples himself in this passage, suggesting that the disciples are supposed to do what he is doing.

In the Qumran texts, one possible reading is that Satan (called Belial) catches people in nets (although this reading is tentative). In Jesusâ usage, the disciples would be fishers who remove people from Satanâs grasp and put them into Godâs net. Perhaps this meaning is linked to the frequent concern with demonic possession in this text, pictured as rescuing people from Satanâs grasp.

The closest HB parallel to this text is Jeremiah 16:16, which is an end-time gathering of people in the context of the judgment of the wicked. Either Jesus is ignoring the context of Jeremiah (because these disciples will bring good news and not catch people for judgment) or he is implying that these disciples will be focused on bringing people to judgment.126In the HB, it is God who fishes for people in judgment (Jer. 16:16), so Jesus is inviting these disciples to be godlike.

In the HB, water is a symbol for chaos, so the fishing that the disciples do will liberate people from chaos.

Because fishing was their livelihood, perhaps Jesus is suggesting that, in one sense, they will be doing what they have always done, but in another sense, they will be doing something entirely different if they follow him. Perhaps the point is that their occupations prepared them for discipleship.

In Ezekiel 47:8â10, the fish can be interpreted as a symbol for redeemed souls living in an area previously barren but now teeming with life. Perhaps this links to the recent imagery of the wilderness to suggest that the disciples will save souls from a barren land.

Perhaps more significant than the precise meaning of âfishersâ is what Jesus did not call them to be: they are not to be students of the law or scribes or experts. The focus will not be on their own knowledge or attainments but on their relationship to others and the heavy labor that they will expend in their calling.

1:18 And straightway they forsook their nets, and followed him: Markâs favorite term (âstraightwayâ) is put to good use here emphasizing the quick obedience of Simon and Andrew.

Some manuscripts have âall thingsâ instead of âtheir nets.â127 The latter is probably the earlier reading, likely changed to make the text more relevant to later Christians.

This call story differs in significant ways from what would have been common in this culture, where it would have been expected for disciples to maintain their trade and to devote time to study. So Markâs early audiences would have seen this as a surprising departure from the standard practice.

It is somewhat common for interpreters to understand âleaving their netsâ as an abandonment of their occupation and wealth, but 1:29 shows Simon and Andrew back in their home, so that interpretation seems to be a misreading. (But see the Analysis on 1:38 for a possible second call to discipleship that would have involved a further break with home and possessions.)

âFollowedâ can have a literal meaning (physically follow) as well as a metaphorical one (follow as a disciple); in reference to leaving the nets, both meanings seem to be intended here. The abrupt and devoted response of Simon and Andrew is evidence of Jesusâ power: âit is, presumably, a mark of the [authority] of Jesus that he could make an outrageous demand without explanation and receive instant obedience.â128

1:19 And when he had gone a little further thence, he saw James the son of Zebedee, and John his brother, who also were in the ship mending their nets: The word âpreparingâ would probably be a better translation than âmending,â as it reflects the verbâs basic meaning of âto put in order.â129 Because fishing was done at night and mending/preparing was done during the day, either some time has passed since Simon and Andrew were called or Mark is taking a small liberty with the chronology, probably to make the contrast between the two callings more vivid.

The New Rendition uses the name âJacobâ instead of âJames.â This translation choice is uncommon but justifiable. The name rendered as âJamesâ130 is a Greek form of the Hebrew name normally translated as âJacob.â While the English name âJamesâ can be traced back to âJacob,â131 modern English speakers are generally unaware of this, and thus the nameâs connection to the HB is lost. This would not have been the case with Markâs original audience: just as names like âSimonâ would have brought to mind figures from Israelâs past, so âJacobâ would have conjured memories of Abrahamâs grandson. Thus, in order to better reflect the Jewish roots of the text and the experience of Markâs early audiences, the New Rendition uses âJacobâ instead of âJames.â

1:20 And straightway he called them: and they left their father Zebedee in the ship with the hired servants, and went after him: Because of the seriousness with which the command to honor father and mother was taken, their actions here would have likely been regarded as a dereliction of duty.

HB Allusions. In 1 Kings 19, Elijah calls Elisha to follow him. Elisha leaves his occupation but asks forâand receivesâpermission to say goodbye to his parents before following Elijah. If this story was on Markâs mind, then Jesus, like Elijah, is a prophet acquiring disciples. But there is also a significant difference in that Jesusâ disciples do not ask for (and do not receive and are not expected to have) permission to say goodbye to their parents before following Jesus, which suggests that following Jesus requires an even greater level of devotion and obedience than following an HB prophet.

These stories of calling disciples are also similar to Exodus 3:1â2, where Moses is called as he works as a shepherd, and Judges 6:11â12, where Gideon is called as he threshes grain. These parallels would emphasize Jesusâ role as an authorized messenger from God and the disciplesâ role as being called from their daily work.

The Two Call Stories Compared. The two stories of Jesus calling disciples are very similar; each contains the following elements:

a participle that shows Jesusâ movement

the verb âhe sawâ

a phrase describing the future disciples going about their daily occupation

Jesusâ call

the disciples follow

a mention of leaving their occupation132

Given how similar these stories are and how brief they are, it is perhaps surprising that Mark did not collapse them into one story. Thus their presentation as two separate stories is likely significant; it seems that Mark intended the audience to compare the two.

One major difference between the two stories stands out: Simon and Andrew are in the water with a netâno boat, no hired hands.133 James and John, on the other hand, are much more prosperous, with a boat and servants. It appears that an important part of Markâs message is that Jesus called his disciples from a variety of socioeconomic positions.

Another difference between the two stories is timing, since fishing was done at night but mending/preparing the nets was done during the day. Perhaps the point of this contrast is to emphasize the universality of Jesusâ call or the fact that, unlike his disciples, Jesus was busy with his âfishingâ (for disciples) both day and night.

There is also some heightening from the first call to the second: Simon and Andrew leave only nets, but James and John leave more prosperous circumstances, not to mention their own father.

Analysis

Given Markâs emphasis on discipleship, it is no accident that the first story of Jesusâ ministry shows Jesus calling disciples. And considering the close proximity of this call story to the note that John the Baptist has been arrested, there is some foreshadowing of the challenges that the disciples will face. The view of discipleship presented in this brief call story is one of instant obedience, even at great personal cost, to a command issued with authority. The socioeconomic contrast between the two calls also hints at themes that will be later developed in Mark more fully.

The summary of Jesusâ teachings in 1:14â15, with its talk of the appointed time and the kingdom of God, leads the audience to expect something absolutely momentous to happen next, but instead, in a classic example of Markâs irony, Jesus asks four rather ordinary men to follow him. This surprising turn of events encourages a reexamination of what exactly the kingdom of God means: its coming is not a dramatic event but rather will be manifested through seemingly ordinary actions. Calling disciples as the inaugural act of Jesusâ ministry is an illustration of the parable of the mustard seed (4:31) as âthe kingdom of God comes not with fanfare but through the gradual gathering of a group of socially insignificant people in an unnoticed corner of provincial Galilee.â134

Coming so soon after two other instances where the audienceâs expectations were dashed (when John preaches of a coming âstrong oneâ but instead a seemingly unimpressive man shows up for baptism, and when the gift of the Holy Spirit doesnât lead to great displays of power but rather a wilderness temptation), Mark continues to show that Jesus will not meet the prevailing expectations.

In other stories from the ancient world where disciples choose to follow a teacher, the story normally emphasizes the disciplesâ reasons for choosing the teacher (such as seeing him perform a miracle). The conspicuous absence of the disciplesâ reasons for following Jesus emphasizes that Jesusâ brief words were filled with power and authority. His authority is also emphasized by the fact that, contrary to normal practice, he chose his disciples; they did not choose him. While the issue of Jesusâ authority is not explicitly raised in this story, it will be in the next one; Mark is doing a bit of foreshadowing and slowly developing this theme.

One of the most common literary techniques used in the Gospel of Mark is sandwiching,135 where one story (the âmeatâ) is surrounded by two similar stories (the two pieces of bread), with the purpose of calling attention to the way that the two relate to each other. In this case, the story of the calling of these four disciples is sandwiched by references to Satan and an unclean spirit. Mark may have been hinting that the process of calling disciples and/or accepting a call to discipleship would be surrounded by satanic efforts. Another signal characteristic of Mark is the use of irony; it is present in this story in the sense that Jesus is âfishingâ for disciples as he calls them to abandon their fishing to become fishers of humans.

21 And they went into Capernaum; and straightway on the sabbath day he entered into the synagogue, and taught.22 And they were astonished at his doctrine: for he taught them as one that had authority, and not as the scribes.23 And there was in their synagogue a man with an unclean spirit; and he cried out,24 Saying, Let us alone; what have we to do with thee, thou Jesus of Nazareth? art thou come to destroy us? New Rendition

21 And they went into Capernaum. And immediately on the Sabbath he taught in the synagogue.22 And they were amazed at his teaching, for he was teaching as one having power/authority and not like the scriptorians.23 And immediately there was in their synagogue a man with an unclean spirit. And he exclaimed,24 saying, âMind your own business, Jesus of Nazareth! Have you come to destroy us? I know who you are: Godâs Holy One!â25 And Jesus stopped him, saying, âSilence! And come out of him.â26 And convulsing him and crying out with a loud voice, the unclean spirit came out of him.27 And all were astonished, so they questioned, saying, âWhat is this new teaching with power/authority? And he even commands the unclean spirits and they obey him.â28 And immediately news about him went out everywhere, in all the region of Galilee.
