King James Version

The Gospel According to St Mark

New Rendition

According to Mark

Notes

the gospel: It is difficult to determine precisely what is meant by the word âgospelâ (Greek: euangelion) because the word underwent two shifts in meaning in the first century:

Before the time of Christianity, the word âgospelâ meant âthe good newsâ of, for example, a military victory,3the birth or ascension of the emperor,4or the festival held on the emperorâs birthday.5In the LXX, âgospelâ refers to the announcement of good news, often without any religious connotation.6If âgospelâ refers to good news, either in the title or in subsequent uses in Markâs Gospel (see 1:14; 8:35; 13:10; 14:9; 16:15), it is theologically significant that this good news is not a military victory or a new earthly king, but rather the ministry of a plain man from Nazareth. âGospelâ may have led the audience to expect the dramatic tale of the ruler of an empire or an impressive battlefield performance, but instead they will find miracles performed to benefit people who live on the margins of society; it is the good news of an entirely different kind of victory.

Later, in the time of the writing of the earliest NT texts, âgospelâ would describe the teachings about Jesus Christ that were transmitted by word of mouth (see, for example, 1 Cor. 9:14; Gal. 1:11; Eph. 1:13). This seems to be the way that this word is used in Paulâs letters (for example, in Rom. 15:19; 1 Cor. 15:1). It is possible that Mark (unintentionally) coined this meaning of the term, although it is more likely that this usage began with Paul.

Still later, âgospelâ would refer to the genre of texts recounting the life of Jesus Christ. This definition would have been in use only after Markâs Gospel was written, so while this meaning might apply to the title of Mark (assuming the title was written sometime after the text; see appendix A: âAuthorship and Dateâ), it probably does not apply to instances of the word within the text itself.

It is worth remembering that the Gospel of Mark is intended to be the good news, even though it will recount Jesus being shunned by family and friends, tortured, mocked, and killed.

Some ancient manuscripts omit the words âThe Gospelâ so that the title is simply âAccording to Mark.â Both versions have a plausible claim to being the earlier title: the shorter title because the tendency was for titles to expand over time and the longer title because the shorter title could have been an abbreviation of it.7

according to: The phrase âaccording toâ may sound unwieldy to modern readers, but it implies that there is only one gospel (that is, only one good news story about Jesus Christ), and this is Markâs rendition of it.

St: The addition of âStâ (the abbreviation for âSaintâ) came about much later than the Gospelâs writing and reflects the practice of canonizing people by conferring sainthood upon them.

Mark: See appendix A: âAuthorship and Dateâ for a discussion of what is known about the author.

Analysis

It is likely that the first copy of the Gospel of Mark had no title at all since, in the first century, titles were more likely to be created by the audience than the author.8 A title would not have been necessary until the Gospel circulated beyond its original location or until there were other written Gospels from which it needed to be distinguished.

Overview of Chapter 1. Mark begins with a prologue, but it is debated whether that prologue ends

after 1:3, which would bracket the HB prophecies from the main text, which makes little reference to fulfillment of HB prophecies.

after 1:8, which would bracket Johnâs ministry from the main text, which is concerned with Jesusâ ministry.

after 1:11, which would bracket Jesusâ baptism from the main text, which is concerned with Jesusâ active ministry.

after 1:13, which would bracket the temptations from the main text; because geography is an important narrative marker in this text, the move from the wilderness to Galilee in 1:14 supports ending the prologue here. Additionally, 1:1â13 has a âheavenly perspectiveâ on Jesusâ ministryâwith HB prophecy, a vision, and temptations known only to Jesusâbut in 1:14, there is a shift to a human perspective on events.

after 1:15, since the word âgospelâ appears in both 1:1 and 1:15, which bookends this section of text and emphasizes its main theme. References to John the Baptist at the beginning (1:4) and end (1:14) of the passage also function as bookends, highlighting the role of John the Baptist in the prologue.

Mark 1:21â45 can be understood as a âtypical dayâ in Jesusâ ministry, showcasing the usual events of a day in Capernaum: teaching, exorcism, healing, and preaching. It is more likely to be a composite presentation than the report of any actual twenty-four-hour day.

King James Version

1 The beginning of the gospel of Jesus Christ, the Son of God;2 As it is written in the prophets, Behold, I send my messenger before thy face, which shall prepare thy way before thee.3 The voice of one crying in the wilderness, Prepare ye the way of the Lord, make his paths straight.4 John did baptize in the wilderness, and preach the baptism of repentance for the remission of sins.5 And there went out unto him all the land of Judaea, and they of Jerusalem, and were all baptized of him in the river of Jordan, confessing their sins.6 And John was clothed with camelâs hair, and with a girdle of a skin about his loins; and he did eat locusts and wild honey;7 And preached, saying, There cometh one mightier than I after me, the latchet of whose shoes I am not worthy to stoop down and unloose.8 I indeed have baptized you with water: but he shall baptize you with the Holy Ghost.

New Rendition

1 The Beginning of the Good News of Jesus, the Anointed One, Son of God

2 As it is written in Isaiah the prophet,

3 A voice shouting in the desert: âMake ready the way of the Lordâ straighten his paths.ââ

4 John appeared, baptizing in the desert, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for forgiveness of sins.5 And all the Judean region and all those of Jerusalem were going out to him; all were being baptized by him in the Jordan River, as they were confessing their sins.6 And John wore clothing made of camelâs hair and a leather belt around his waist, and he was eating grasshoppers and wild honey.7 And he proclaimed, saying, âOne who is more powerful than I comes following me, of whom I am not worthy to stoop to untie the strap of his sandals.8 I baptized you with water, but he will baptize you with the Holy Spirit.â

Notes

1:1 The beginning of the gospel: The word âtheâ is lacking in the Greek text, perhaps indicating that this verse functioned as a title or heading.9

âBeginningâ can mean

a moment in time.This use would then indicate the moment in time when the gospel began. The three other uses of âbeginningâ in Markâs Gospel (see 10:6; 13:8; and 13:19) use âbeginningâ in this sense.

fundamentals.The Gospel of Mark would then be presenting the basic story and message of the life of Jesus.

first exposure.10The Gospel of Mark would thus be the first presentation of the life of Jesus for an audience.

rule or norm.Jesusâ life would thus be presented as a pattern for the audience to follow.

of Jesus Christ: This âofâ can be understood in two different ways: the gospel is proclaimed by Jesus Christ or the gospel is about Jesus Christ. It may encompass both meanings (âthe good news by and about Jesus Christâ).

âJesusâ is the Greek form of the Hebrew name âJoshuaâ and was one of the most common names for first-century Jewish men.11 It is perhaps ironic that Jesus had such a common name when he lived a singular life.

âChristâ is the Greek equivalent of the Hebrew word âMessiahâ and can be translated into English as âthe anointed one,â which reflects the underlying meaning of the Hebrew term. It was initially a title (âJesus the Messiahâ or âJesus the Anointed Oneâ) and only later functioned as a proper name (âJesus Christâ). It is possible that by the time the Gospel of Mark was written, it already would have been considered a proper name, but it may still have been a title. This verse is the only time that the phrase âJesus Christâ appears in Markâs Gospel.

the Son of God: Whether this phrase was in the earliest copies of Mark is disputed since some ancient manuscripts omit the words âthe Son of Godâ here.12 There are two possibilities for what has happened:

The phrase was originally in the text, but a scribeâs eye skipped from the word âChristâ to the word âGodâ (since both words end with the same two letters in Greek), and so it was accidentally omitted.

âThe Son of Godâ was not originally in the text but was added later for one of these reasons:

This verse seems to have functioned as a title, and there is a trend for expanding the titles of texts.13

There is also a tendency to elaborate titles for Christ. In fact, some later manuscripts read âChrist, the Son of the living Godâ here.14

The phrase may have been added later to counter the belief that Jesus was not the Son of God until his baptism by noting that he was called âthe Son of Godâ even before he was baptized.15

The theme of Jesus as Godâs son is an important one to Mark, and the Gospel will have a lot to say about it: the title âSon of Godâ will appear again, and at crucial junctures, in Markâs Gospel (see 1:11; 3:11; 5:7; 9:7; 12:6â8; 14:61â62; 15:39). While it is well established in Markâs Gospel that Jesus Christ is the Son of God, that does not necessarily mean that this phrase was originally in this particular verse. But if it was included here, it serves as an important identity marker for Jesus since Markâs early audiences would have understood that calling Jesus âthe Son of Godâ implied his âpreexistence and deityâ;16 other interpreters reject this association, pointing out that the HB uses it for mortal kings.17 There may be a distinction here based on audience, with Jews thinking that a mortal could be Godâs son but Gentiles thinking that âSon of Godâ implied a divine status.18

This verse does not contain a verb, perhaps indicating that it functioned as a heading, either for the entire text or for part of it (that is, just for the âbeginningâ of the gospel). The meaning would differ based on what exactly it is introducing:

If it introduces 1:2â3, it means that âthe beginning of the gospel of Jesus Christâ consists of the prophecies of HB prophets. This would suggest that the gospel began with the HB prophets.

If âthe beginningâ extends to 1:8, then Johnâs ministry is the beginning of the gospel. Reading Johnâs ministry this way is intriguing because of the possibility (see the Notes on 1:7) that Jesus was Johnâs disciple.

If it includes 1:11, then Jesusâ baptism is part of the beginning. This would frame Jesusâ baptism as well as the practice of baptism as part of âthe beginning.â

If it includes 1:13, then the temptations in the wilderness are part of the beginning of the gospel. Because the temptations are dissimilar to any other event in Jesusâ life, it makes sense to bracket it as being part of the introduction.

If it includes 1:15, then it contains the summary of Jesusâ preaching. Thus the beginning of the gospel includes all of the preparatory events as well as an overview of Jesusâ message.

If it introduces the entire Gospel, then the first half (1:2â8:29) explains how Jesus is the Christ and climaxes with Peterâs statement âThou art the Christâ (8:29), and the second half (8:30â16:8) explains how Jesus is the Son of God and climaxes with the centurionâs statement âTruly this man was the Son of Godâ (15:39). While this is possible, it is less likely since Markâs story seems to be divided into thirdsânot into halves (see the overview above).

It is possible that this verse was not originally part of the Gospel of Mark (although it is included in the extant manuscripts) because

it does not contain a verb, suggesting that it functioned as a title; titles were not usually written by the author.

it is not included in either Matthewâs or Lukeâs texts, and, since both wrote their Gospels with Markâs text in hand, it is likely that this verse was not included in their copies of Mark.

there are many variants in the wording of this verse in the various ancient manuscripts; often, variants arise when the text is unsure or was deemed in some way defective.

it seems to use a meaning of âgospelâ (written text) that postdates Mark.

it uses âJesus Christâ as a proper name, which is not done elsewhere in Mark and probably postdates Mark.

the âasâ in 1:2 normally follows other material and is a subordinate clause lacking a main clause, so a scribe might have wanted to insert text to give it something to follow. It is possible that there was other material, later lost, at the beginning of the gospel, leaving the inadequate âasâ in 1:2 and leading a scribe to compose 1:1 to fill the gap.

it simply indicates âhere begins the Gospel of Mark,â inserted to distinguish it from the Gospel before it when the Gospels were collected into one written text.19

1:2 As it is written in the prophets: The verb tense may indicate how Mark thought about scripture: âthe Greek perfect tense (âhas been writtenâ), which implies a past action with permanent results, is particularly appropriate for such a formula, since it suggests that the ancient writing is not just a dead letter but a living force in the present.â20

This is the standard formula used in the NT to introduce biblical quotations. What is unusual about Markâs usage is that this is the only time Mark uses this formula and the formula normally follows after a story and shows how it fulfills HB prophecy.21

There is an intriguing tension between the idea of Jesusâ life as ânewâ and the idea that it was prophesied of hundreds of years in advance.

Instead of âin the prophets,â many of the earliest Greek texts read âin Isaiah the prophet.â There are several possibilities for what has happened here:

The phrase was originally âin Isaiah the prophet,â but given that the quotation that follows is a combination of Exodus 23:20, Malachi 3:1, and Isaiah 40:3, later scribesârealizing the referent was incorrectâchanged it to âin the prophets.â22From a Latter-day Saint perspective, there is no problem viewing âin Isaiah the prophetâ as an example of the âmistakes of menâ23that might be found in a text.

Ancient texts can have multiple iterations (compare Mal. 4; 3 Ne. 25; D&C 98:16â17; and especially JSâH 1:36â39); it is theoretically possible that Mark had access to a copy of Isaiah that did indeed contain all of the material quoted in this verse, which would make âin Isaiah the prophetâ a correct description of the quotation and âin the prophetsâ an inaccurate âcorrectionâ made by a later hand.

Markâs Gospel as originally written only included the quotation from Isaiah, and a later copyist inserted the quotations from Exodus and Malachi.

Mark was following a customary practice of naming only the most important of his sources and not all of them;24Isaiah was much better known than Malachi.

Isaiah is named here because he was the source of the majority of the quotation.

The idea of being âwritten in Isaiahâ refers more generally to the themes in 1:1â15 and not to the quote here in particular. Many themes from Isaiah play out throughout Mark in general and in this first section of text in particular.

Behold, I send my messenger before thy face: The purpose of the word âbeholdâ is to focus the audienceâs attention on the coming material. It could be translated as âpay attention.â25

The âIâ here refers to God.

âMessengerâ is the same word (Greek: angelos) that is translated as âangelâ (for example, 1:13; 12:25; 13:32). There are several possibilities for the identity of the messenger:

In Exodus 23:20, which might be quoted here, the messenger is an angel sent from God to lead the people to the promised land.

The messenger from Malachi 3:1 (which also may be quoted here) is identified as Elijah (Mal. 4:5), whose job it is to prepare the earth so it will not be cursed when the Lord returns. Because Elijah did not die but was taken up to heaven (2 Kgs. 2:11), his return was expected.

The messenger might be John, who prepares the way of Jesus Christ, as the context of Markâs first chapter suggests.

The messenger might be Jesus, preparing the way for Godâs reign.

It is also possible that more than one meaning was intended. In that case, Mark is relying on the multiple possible meanings of this verse in order to encourage his audience to see the prophecy fulfilled in more than one manner.

Many modern translations read âahead of youâ (NIV and NRSV) here instead of âbefore thy faceâ; âthy faceâ is probably an overly literal translation of an idiom meaning âin your presence.â The word âthyâ could refer either to Jesus (which makes sense grammatically since he was named in 1:1) or to the nation of Israel (which is the meaning in the context of Ex. 23:20). Perhaps both meanings are intended, which has the effect of making Jesus Christ the personified nation of Israelâan idea that might also be present at Jesusâ baptism (see the Analysis of 1:9â15). Once again, the multiple possible layers of meaning allow Mark to convey multiple messages to the audience.

The New Rendition presents this quotation from Isaiah as poetry (that is, written in lines instead of in paragraph form) to better reflect Isaiahâs writing style.

which shall prepare thy way before thee: The type of âpreparationâ envisioned here depends on who is doing the preparing and for whom they are doing it:

In the Exodus 23:20 context, an angel prepares the way for the people.

In Malachi 3:1, the messenger prepares for the return of the Lord.

Mark may be borrowing scriptural language but applying it to a different context.

The customary interpretation of this text is that John the Baptist is preparing the way for Jesus. Support for this reading comes from 9:11â13, where Elijah and John the Baptist are linked. This preparation would involve preparing people, through repentance and baptism, for Jesusâ ministry. It is also possible that Johnâs ministry and martyrdom prepared the way for Jesus in the sense of showing Jesus (or others) the pattern for Jesusâ own life.

âThyâ can refer to:

In Exodus 23:20, the peopleâs way is being prepared, so âthe peopleâ would be the referent for âthy.â

This text quotes Malachi 3:1, but where Malachi has âmy way,â Mark has âthy way.â The likely purpose of this shift is to associate Jesus with the Lord of the HB, which would have been a startling idea to those new to the Christian message.

But again, Mark may be borrowing the language but changing the referents or relying on the possibility that the language can carry multiple referents.

There are two options for understanding the meaning of âwayâ:

âWayâ is a physical road. It is uncertain if in the HB this was intended literally or metaphorically, but in Mark a more metaphorical usage is intended.

âThe Wayâ is a name for the early Christian movement;26it is difficult to know if this meaning existed when Mark was writing and so could have been intended here, or if it was only used later.

The best Greek manuscripts lack the phrase âbefore theeâ; it seems to have been added later to harmonize with the accounts in Matthew 11:10 and Luke 7:27.27

Relationship to Malachi 3:1 and Exodus 23:20. Mark quotes Malachi 3:1 (âBehold, I will send my messenger, and he shall prepare the way before meâ). The rest of Malachi 3:1 reads, âand the Lord, whom ye seek, shall suddenly come to his temple, even the messenger of the covenant, whom ye delight in: behold, he shall come, saith the Lord of hosts.â Mark omits the reference to the temple; there are several possible reasons for this:

The temple was not particularly relevant to Markâs audienceâeither because of their location, their lack of ties to Judaism, or because the temple had been destroyedâso Mark de-emphasizes its role.

Mark wants to emphasize that John is offering forgiveness independent of the temple rituals and therefore sees no need to mention it. It is even possible that the lack of reference to it where it would have been expected was meant to convey that the temple was, in effect, deliberately missing from the story.

In chapters 11â13, the corruptness of the temple leadership will take center stage; Mark lays the groundwork for that theme here by de-emphasizing the temple.

While it is not quoted, Mark does intend for the audience to apply the prophecy that the Lord will come to the temple; this prophecy will be fulfilled when Jesus enters the temple (11:15). In this case, the omission would not be because Mark is downplaying the role of the temple, but rather it would be evidence of an allusive writing style.

Malachi was generally regarded by first-century Jews as the last of the prophets, so it would have been appropriate to use his words to introduce Godâs new messengers, John and Jesus. Malachiâs message, as taken up by Mark, is clear: God is about to begin acting in history again.

It is possible that Exodus 23:20 (KJV: âBehold, I send an angel before thee, to keep thee in the way, and to bring thee into the place which I have preparedâ) is quoted here as well.

As indicated above, there are various ways to interpret whom Mark intends to fill each role:

I (God) send my messenger (John) before your (Jesusâ) face. This reading features God speaking to Jesus and making promises to him, making this quite an important moment. The audience for Markâs Gospel would enjoy a privileged position as they âoverheardâ this statement.

I (God) send my messenger (Jesus) to go before your (the audienceâs) face. This reading makes sense of Jesus going before the disciples (10:32), crying out on the cross (15:34), and going before the disciples to Galilee (14:28; 16:7). In this view, this HB quotation becomes a summation for Jesusâ entire mortal ministry.

I (God) send my messenger (Mark) before your (the audienceâs) face who makes a path (writing the gospel) for you.28In this reading, the audience is invited to see themselves as playing a role foretold in scripture.

While the first reading is traditional, the other two are also possible; perhaps more than one level of meaning was implied.

1:3 The voice of one crying in the wilderness: The verb âcryingâ does not mean âweepingâ but rather shouting or proclaiming.

In LXX Isaiah 40:3 (which is quoted here), the phrase âin the wildernessâ modifies the previous phrase (âthe voice in the wildernessâ), but in the Hebrew text of Isaiah 40:3, it modifies the following phrase (âprepare in the wildernessâ).

Contra the modern Western impression of âthe wildernessâ as a forest, in the Bible it was a desert: âan extraordinarily barren and arid area riven by canyons between cliffs filled with caves, rocky and sun-blasted.â29 While it is possible that the wildernessâbarren, harsh, and dangerousâwas considered a terrifying place filled with demons, it is more likely a positive symbol since it was there that the Lord met and guided the people of Israel (in what is the prime Jewish association with wilderness), and it was prophesied to be where God would make a new beginning with the covenant people (Isa. 40:3â5; 41:18â19; 43:19â21; Ezek. 20:35â38; Hosea 2:14â15), a theme particularly appropriate to the context of chapter 1. The Essene community established themselves at Qumran (which is in the wilderness of Judea) precisely in order to fulfill this expectation; they thought that God would visit them there and would support their stances against the corruption in Jerusalem.30 It was only later in Christian thought that the wilderness became a symbol for hostile forces. Interestingly, despite the multiple references to the wilderness in chapter 1, there are only two references to it in the rest of the Gospel, in chapter 6.

Prepare ye the way of the Lord: This phrase does not use the same Greek word that was translated as âprepareâ in 1:2.31 This verb is plural, indicating that more than one person is expected to prepare. Repeating the idea of âpreparationâ from the previous verse (even using a different word) implies that John prepares the way for Jesus by urging other people to prepare.

The âofâ here means âthe path that the Lord will use,â not âthe path that the Lord ownsâ or âthe path that the Lord prepared.â

This text is presented as poetry in the New Rendition since the Isaiah text that it is quoting is poetry.

make his paths straight: This phrase suggests that everything possible is done to make his way easy, since a straight path would be easier to follow.

Relationship to Isaiah 40:3. Mark 1:3 quotes Isaiah 40:3 (âThe voice of him that crieth in the wilderness, Prepare ye the way of the Lord, make straight in the desert a highway for our Godâ), in a version closer to the LXX than to the Hebrew.

The âwayâ in Isaiah 40:3 is not how the Lord wants people to walk, but the way that the Lord himself will walk: âhis triumphant march through the wilderness and into the holy city as he leads his people back from exile in a magnificent demonstration of saving power.â32

Both the Hebrew and the LXX of Isaiah 40:3 read âour Godâ where Mark has âhisâ in reference to the âpath.â The change from âGodâ to âhisâ (which presumably refers to Jesus) in Mark makes a link between Jesus and God. So not only is Mark quoting HB scriptures about preparation here, he is also teaching about Jesusâ relationship to the God of the HB.

Both the Exodus/Malachi scripture in the previous verse and the Isaiah scripture in this verse are about a figure sent to lead an exodus through the wilderness. By quoting both,33 Mark strongly emphasizes that theme as the introduction to the story of Jesus. But there is quite a contrast between the Exodus/Malachi quotation and the Isaiah quotation: the Isaiah material strikes a hopeful note and envisions a positive event, while the context in Malachi is one of judgment and punishment. So by combining them, there is a balance of the positive (hope) and negative (judgment).

The reference to Isaiah in 1:2 and the quote from Isaiah in 1:3 form bookends around the Exodus/Malachi quotation; if this structure was intentional, then it highlights the idea of Isaiah as a prophetic witness to the gospel story.

Jesusâ Identity. While these verses might initially seem to be about the role of John the Baptist, it is also possible to read them with an eye to what they convey about Jesus:34

They suggest that his ministry was prophesied of in the HB.

They equate him with the God of the HB.

They introduce his ministry as nested within Johnâs ministry and within HB expectations.

These are very important theological principles and are quite the introduction to Jesus, especially considering that Jesus has not yet appeared in the text.

1:4 John did baptize in the wilderness: Mark presents no introduction to John here (except the HB quotations), either because Markâs audience already knew who John was35 or because his background would not have fit Markâs narrative purposes, which emphasize discipleship over lineage. This abrupt introduction presents John as the prophesied voice in the wilderness who, in the HB sources, is not given a background either. So the lack of background, ironically, becomes a background: it strengthens the association between John and the voice crying in the wilderness that had been prophesied in the HB. Perhaps Mark thought that any other information about John would have diluted that message.

Before the NT, the verb âbaptizeâ meant to dip or to plunge. Only in the NT does it take on a technical meaning with reference to a religious ritual. The precedent36 for Johnâs baptism is debated:

In the HB, priests washed themselves before participating in temple rituals, as did regular worshipers if they had become impure (which would have happened regularly),37but there are some differences between this practice and Johnâs baptism:

In the HB, one immersed oneselfâno one performed the action for them.

The washings were required repeatedly, not just once.

There was no connotation of changing oneâs mind (the Greek word that the KJV translates as ârepentanceâ connotes changing oneâs mind).

A ritual bath became one of the requirements for conversion to Judaism, but the date of this practice is unknown and perhaps only occurred after Johnâs time. This practice differed from Johnâs baptism in that:

People apparently immersed themselves.

It was only for Gentiles who were converting, not Jews.

In Qumran, a baptism associated with repentance was performed as a ritual immersion in a desert locale38(although it is possible that this was metaphorical and not a literal practice). This differed from Johnâs practice because the baptism was repeated, not done only once.

Given what was likely known to John and his audience (and to Mark and the Gospelâs audience) about practices similar to Johnâs baptism, the following innovations are important:

If the HB practice of purity washings is in view, then the suggestion is that purity doesnât have to do with ritual status but rather with internal moral condition. The focus of Johnâs baptism is on repentance, not ritual purity. This would be a profound shift, particularly given Markâs emphasis on the idea that Johnâs ministry prepared the way for the Lord.

If convert baptism is in view, then John is making quite a claim to suggest that the covenant people required something akin to a conversion in order to be forgiven of their sins.

Under the Mosaic law, forgiveness of sins was to be found in the performance of sacrificial temple rituals. So Johnâs suggestion that remission of sins could be foundwithoutperforming temple sacrifices was a radical and innovative idea.

If John is introducing the idea that baptism requires someone else to perform it against a backdrop where people were used to performing their own ritual purifications, then that is quite the innovation. While specifically Latter-day Saint ideas of authority and ordinances are not explored in this text, Johnâs practice would be a profound commentary on the need for an authorized person to perform an ordinance. This innovation may make sense of why it is that John needs to baptize Jesus: it emphasizes that Christian baptism is not something that one does for oneself, but rather something that someone elseâsomeone with authorityâdoes. It might even begin to hint at the idea of an atonement, where someone else performs something one cannot do for oneself.

There is no suggestion that John would baptize people more than once; this appears to be a one-time-only ritual. Given the contrast to the washings under the law of Moses, there may be a hint of a final âgreat and last sacrificeâ (Alma 34:14) in the idea that rituals (sacrifices for forgiveness, washings for purity) that were repeated many times were no longer needed.

Perhaps the (perceived) novelty of the baptism that he offered explains why John is one of the rare persons in scripture known by a title such as âthe Baptistâ (there is no âMoses the Anointerâ or âElijah the Sealer,â but see also 1 Chr. 29:29).

The fact that John is pictured baptizing in the wilderness requires consideration, as it is not the most logical place to draw a crowd. The purpose of baptizing âin the wildernessâ is likely more theological than practical:

Johnâs ministry is associated with the time of trial and learning that Israel spent in the wilderness.

Johnâs ministry is associated with messianic movements, which were often located in the wilderness.

John fulfilled the HB prophecies that Mark has just referenced that envisioned a preparer in the wilderness.

John fulfilled a general theme in the HB regarding the wilderness: âessential to the prophetic concern with repentance in Hosea, Amos and Isaiah is the concept of Israelâs time in the wilderness as the period of true sonship to God, a status into which the Lord is going to lead his people once again in a future time.â39

and preach the baptism of repentance for the remission of sins: In modern English, âpreachâ can have a negative connotation40 which can be avoided by translating âproclaimâ here.

âRepentanceâ means âto change oneâs mind.â The word is used not only in religious contexts but also more generally in the extant literature. Possible meanings here include the following:

Baptism was an effort to get God to ârepentâ (change the mind) about judging the people as wicked: âthere is a biblical tradition that Godâs decisions regarding punishments and blessings are conditional and may be altered.â41

Outside of the Bible, ârepentâ meant âregret for individual acts.â42This is similar to the modern Latter-day Saint usage of the word. So those seeking Johnâs baptism may have been expressing contrition over their actions.

âTo repentâ can be understood as âto return,â a common idea in the HB where a wayward people are frequently pictured as being invited by prophets to return to God. Compared with option 2, this is a more community-oriented approach to repentance. This reading makes sense of the idea of a âpathâ or âwayâ being prepared, although there is a bit of irony in the fact that it is God who uses the way to return to the people and not the people who use it to return to God.

The word âremissionâ is used for the release of debts or, more generally, sending something away. It is perhaps misleading to modern English speakers who normally associate it with a disease that is under control but not cured; that meaning would be inaccurate here since forgiveness conveys the idea that the sin has been completely eradicated.

There are several possibilities43 for understanding how the terms baptism, repentance, and remission relate to each other:

The relationship is chronological: first baptism, then repentance, then the remission of sins.

Baptism itself could have the effect of remitting sins.

Baptism could be evidence of remitted sins.

Baptism could be a symbol that one is seeking remission of sins.

Repentance causes the remission of sins.

The reference to repentance ensures that baptism isnât viewed as a sort of magical act that washes away sins by itself (which would be one possible reading of #2 above), but rather as the outward sign of an inward commitment, with remission of sins as the result.

The âofâ describeswhat kindof baptism this wasânot for membership in a community or for ritual purification, but rather a ârepentance baptism.â

There are at least three ways to understand the relationship between 1:2â3 (the HB references) and 1:4 (the description of Johnâs ministry):

Johnâs ministryâfocused on repentance and baptismâprepared the way of the Lord by priming the people to hear the gospel message.

Those who repented and were baptized were ready to learn about and from Jesusâthey were prepared for the Lord.

Johnâs ministry prepared the way of the Lord by setting the stage for Jesusâ own baptism.

Regardless of which option best explains the relationship between the HB prophecies and Johnâs ministry, the fulfillment of the HB prophecies is something other than strictly literal. (That is, John did not create a road in the desert for Jesus to walk upon, but he did engage in the very real action of preparing the âwayâ for the Lord.) If more than one of these options is thought to be reasonable, then not only is the fulfillment not strictly literal, but it is also multilayered; this suggests a certain approach to HB prophecy that sees the fulfillment of those prophecies as very real but not literal.

Relationship to Ezekiel 36:25â28. This text from Ezekiel has the following similarities to Markâs presentation of Johnâs ministry:

A water ritual for purification.

Reference to the role of the Spirit.

Covenant language.

Prophecy of a future action of the Lord.

If Mark understood Johnâs ministry to be the fulfillment of this prophecy, then it suggests that Mark wanted to emphasize the idea of a new covenant, which is the main theme of the passage in Ezekiel.

1:5 And there went out unto him all the land of JudÃ¦a, and they of Jerusalem: The imperfect verb tense (new Rendition: âwere going outâ) implies a stream of people over a period of time, not a one-time event.

Because Jerusalem was in Judea, the phrasing here is unusual. Markâs point may have been to emphasize the âreverse exodusâ of people from Jerusalem into the wilderness or to make a connection to Zechariah 13:1.

and were all baptized of him in the river of Jordan: It is virtually certain that not âallâ of Judea or Jerusalem were baptized by John, so this statement requires a bit of probing:

Josephus notes that many people sought out John,44so this may be hyperbole, but it is not fabrication. As hyperbole, it emphasizes Johnâs popularity. And if John is incredibly popular, that casts Johnâs statement about his unworthiness relative to Jesus as all the more powerful. Reading the âallâ as an instance of Markâs penchant for hyperbole is the most common reading of this passage.

The focus is on the repentance and baptism of the entire community, not on the proportion of individuals who were baptized.

The âallâ could refer to people from a variety of classesâit would then not be hyperbole but literally true that people from all walks of life sought Johnâs baptism.

There may be an allusion to

Zechariah 13:1, where all the people of Jerusalem will be washed in a future day to become ritually pure. As background, this would again illustrate that Johnâs ministry is a fulfillment of prophecy.

Jonah 3:5, where the entire city of Nineveh repents. If this is the allusion that Mark has in mind, it is a powerful indictment of Jerusalem to associate them with wicked Nineveh.

âOf himâ emphasizes that John performed the baptisms. This might seem like a mundane observation to modern readers, but it actually implies quite a departure from standard practice on Johnâs part.

The fact that they are baptized in a river and that Jesus is described as coming up out of the water implies that this was baptism by immersion.

While not apparent in the KJV, the Greek text has a small chiastic structure with references to all, Judea, Jerusalem, and then âallâ again. This pattern emphasizes the idea that âallâ people went out to be baptized by John.

confessing their sins: It is likely too wooden a reading to see the baptism as happening at precisely the same time as the confession, but the Greek text does link the two events. There are several different ways to understand how the confession worked here:

They confess their sins to themselves, perhaps literally at the moment of baptism.

They confess to John; this would be another indication of Johnâs authority.

They confess their sins to God. This would be an interesting inversion of what happens at Jesusâ baptism: the people speak to God when they are baptized, but when Jesus is baptized, God speaks to him.

The act of baptism itself was considered a confession of sins; no other confession is envisioned.

Relationship to 2 Kings 5:1â19. Naaman is invited by the prophet Elisha to bathe in the Jordan River to heal his leprosy. Mark 1 echoes that story through references to water, the Jordan River, purification, and the presence of a prophetic figure. But there is also a critical edge to the comparison: Naaman is a Gentile, so the people coming âout of Jerusalemâ to be baptized are filling the narrative role of a Gentile, something that would have been shocking to most first-century Jewish sensibilities. The association between the stories also positions John as a prophet, as Elisha was, asking the people to do something apparently simple that would have profound results.

Theme: A Reverse Exodus. âWent outâ (Greek: ekporeuomai) is the same word used to describe the Exodus (Ex. 13:4, 8; Deut. 23:5; Josh. 2:10), so there might be Exodus imagery here.45 But since the people are going out from Jerusalem into the wilderness, it reverses the original Exodus journey: Jerusalem has become the new Egypt from which the covenant people need to flee. Being baptized may be, at least symbolically, akin to crossing the Red Sea in the Exodus narrative, especially since âthe Jordan River also has symbolic significance as the site of the miraculous entrance of the tribes of Israel into the promised land, an event analogous to the miraculous crossing of the Red Sea.â46 The sequence of motion from Jerusalem, into the wilderness, and (symbolically) across the water suggests a move backwards in history, returning to the âoldâ relationship with God. But at the same time, the reversal of direction suggests something new, creating a compelling tension between the old and the new.

1:6 And John was clothed with camelâs hair, and with a girdle of a skin about his loins: Some ancient manuscripts lack the phrase âwith a girdle of a skin about his loins.â47 Perhaps this phrase was added by a copyist, but it is also possible that it was originally present and was omitted when a scribeâs eye accidentally skipped from one word to a similar one.

A âgirdle of a skin about his loinsâ refers to a leather belt worn around the waist. The belt could have held money or other itemsâin 6:8, the same word (Greek: zÅnÄ) is translated as âpurseââand would have been used to tuck in the robe (in this case, it was made out of camel hair, which would have been roughly woven and coarse) for ease of movement.

Because John has already been introduced and the story has moved on to the baptism of the people, this description of Johnâs clothing seems awkwardly placed, but it calls attention to his clothing in a way that mentioning it when he was first introduced would not have. His clothing has rich symbolic associations (see the section âRelationship to 2 Kings 1:1â8â below).

and he did eat locusts and wild honey: These were typical foods for the wilderness. There may be a symbolic element to Johnâs diet, as there is with his clothing:

John has, at least metaphorically, escaped Adamâs curse by not having to work by the sweat of his brow in order to eat; he eats what is freely available. This would emphasize the theme of a new creation in chapter 1.

John has rejected human community and only eats what he can get for himself. This would emphasize the wickedness and corruptness of society.

Because these foods would have been easily accessible in the wilderness, John would have been able to focus on his ministry. The simple food may indicate that John was an ascetic.

A diet of locusts and wild honey would have made observance of purity laws simple for John since these foods âwould not have been handled by anyone else, and wild honey would not be subject to tithing.â48

The HB links wild honey to Godâs care for Israel (for example, Ex. 3:8; Deut. 32:13; and Ps. 81:16). Perhaps Johnâs diet, like his clothes, is one of the ways that he preaches. The clothing sends a message about Johnâs identity, while the diet sends a message about Godâs provision for the covenant people.

These typical wilderness foods strengthen the association between John and the HB prophecies about the wilderness that opened the text.

While John dresses like Elijah, he does not eat like Elijah: Elijah was fed by ravens (1 Kgs. 17:6). Maybe Johnâs diet teaches that while John fulfills the role of Elijah, he is not literally Elijah.

Relationship to 2 Kings 1:1â8. In this text, the messengers of King Ahaziah encounter a man who gives them a prophetic message. When the king later asks them to identify the man, they describe a hairy man with a leather belt. The king instantly knows that this must have been Elijah. In other words, Elijah was identifiable by his appearance; a leather belt and camelâs hair were not normal clothing in the wilderness. So when John dresses as Elijah (substituting camelâs hair for natural hairiness), he becomes instantly identifiable as Elijah, almost as if he were wearing a costume. The association between a prophet and hairy clothing [KJV: ârough garmentâ] is also made in Zechariah 13:4.

Why would John have done this?49 Perhaps to encourage the association between his own ministry and that of Elijahâs:

Elijah preaches repentance (1 Kgs. 18:21).

Elijah is linked to the wilderness (1 Kgs. 17:3; 19:3â18).

Elijahâs ministry is linked to the Jordan (2 Kgs. 2:4â11).

Elijahâs ministry ends as his successor, Elisha, receives a âdouble portionâ of the prophetic spirit (2 Kgs. 2:4â11); this is similar to the ending of Johnâs ministry as well as the idea taught by John that Jesus would be âmightierâ than he was.

Because they believed that Elijah would return (Mal. 4:5), the audience would have been primed to see John in the role of Elijah.50 The stage has been set for God to act in history and for ancient prophecies to be fulfilled. There is a compelling tension between the fact that Mark has promised the audience âgood newsâ while the texts that speak of Elijahâs return (Mal. 4:5) speak of the end times.

1:7 And preached, saying, There cometh one mightier than I after me: Through this preaching, John is the voice crying in the wilderness known from the HB prophecies. The syntax here suggests something about the preaching: âThe emphatic position of this futuristic present (á¼ÏÏÎµÏÎ±Î¹, erchetai) should be noted. Placed first in the sentence, it underscores the urgency of Johnâs message.â51

The oblique phrasing of âone mightier than Iâ may suggest that John himself was not entirely sure of the identity of the coming one (Would it be God? The Messiah? The Son of Man?) at this point;52 he only knew that someone was coming and that that person had more strength (or authority) than he had. It might also imply that John is aware of some lack in his own authorityâa lack that the coming one will fill. Once again, while modern Latter-day Saint notions of the priesthood are not present in the Gospel of Mark, this does not imply that similar themes are not explored.

This statement combines with the three HB quotations above to emphasize that someone important is coming. Given that John is presented as the fulfillment of HB prophecies and as someone so popular that âallâ of Judea went to him, the fact that he is preaching that someone who is stronger than even he is coming is quite extraordinary and sets up very high expectations for who this coming person will be. Because this prophecy will be fulfilled almost instantly (1:9), it adds to the audienceâs ability to trust John.

The word âafterâ can have two different meanings:

Time (that is, âhe will come at a later timeâ). In Markâs Gospel, there seems to be a deliberate structuring of the text to imply that Jesusâ public ministry happens only after John the Baptist is imprisoned (1:14).

Position (that is, âfollowing behind meâ) with the sense of following as a disciple (1:17, 20; 8:34) and perhaps even as a technical term for discipleship. Thus Jesus would be Johnâs disciple. While this concept might initially seem unusual, several ideas recommend it:

It is consistent with the idea that Jesus grew in wisdom (Luke 2:40, but note that Mark does not have a parallel text).

It emphasizes Jesusâ humility.

The reference to Jesus as Johnâs disciple is made in the context of pointing out that John knows that Jesus will be greater than he is, instantly eliminating any sense that John was greater than Jesus just because Jesus was Johnâs disciple.

Filling the role of disciple would be part of Jesus setting an example for all people.

Having been a disciple himself, Jesus would then be able to have disciples of his own while still showing humility. He would thus be following the pattern John set of having disciples while still being humble.

It illustrates how John prepared the way of the Lord by being his teacher and letting Jesus be his disciple.

The metaphor about untying sandals (see the Notes on 1:7 below) may by itself indicate that Jesus was Johnâs disciple. This metaphor also makes it clear that the disciple will surpass the teacher.

The fact that Jesus sought out John for baptism might itself be evidence that Jesus was Johnâs disciple.

the latchet of whose shoes I am not worthy to stoop down and unloose: The thong (KJV: âlatchetâ) of the sandals (KJV: âshoesâ) was a length of leather that bound the sandal to the foot.

Some manuscripts lack the phrase âto stoop down,â probably under the influence of the parallel accounts in Luke 3:16 and John 1:27.53 But if that phrase was in the earliest texts of Mark, note that John doesnât only say that he is unworthy to untie Jesusâ sandal but also that he is unworthy to stoop down to do it; perhaps there is a connection to Malachi 3:2 (a text close to the HB quotations that began Markâs Gospel), which asks who will be able to âstandâ when the Lord comes. If this is the case, John is making the point that not only will he not âstand,â he wonât even be worthy to stoop.

In rabbinic teaching, a disciple should perform for the teacher every task that a slave would for his master except untying his sandals;54 the disciple was exempted from this particular task because it was thought to be too demeaning. In some expressions of Jewish tradition, a Hebrew slave was not obligated to attend to his masterâs shoes.55 If these customs are the background to Johnâs statement, then he is saying that he is not only unworthy to be Jesusâ disciple, but he is also unworthy to be his slave. Given the efforts Mark has taken to make John appear prominent (as the fulfillment of HB prophecies) and popular (as one who draws enormous crowds), Johnâs statement of his unworthiness relative to Jesus is very powerful. Given that these are Johnâs first spoken words in the text, the fact that they so powerfully attest to Jesusâ superiority over him signals both his own humility and Jesusâ high status.

Johnâs statement might also explain why Jesus would have sought out what has been identified as a âbaptism of repentanceâ: Johnâs expression of his unworthiness relative to Jesus would ensure that his audience (and Markâs audience) did not regard Jesus as in need of baptism.

John is a Moses figure who leads the peopleâbut not all the wayâinto the promised land. Jesus, like Joshua (with whom he shares a name since âJesusâ is the Greek form of the Hebrew âJoshuaâ), finishes the journey with the people. This reading makes sense of Johnâs baptism as preparatory.

1:8 I indeed have baptized you with water: âIndeedâ is not present in the Greek text as a separate word but was probably included in the KJV to indicate that the âIâ is emphasized in Greek. The point of Johnâs statement is thus more about the contrast between John and Jesus (which is also the point of the previous verse) than the difference between water and Holy Spirit baptism. The emphasis on John as baptizer is also significant given that most rituals similar to his baptism (ritual washings, etc.) were self-administered; here, John calls attention to the fact that he performed baptisms.

The word translated as âhave baptizedâ is in the perfect tense, which could imply one of the following:

John has just baptized the people to whom he is speaking. His words might be understood differently if they were addressed to people who have already been baptized: perhaps learning about Jesus (as the coming one who is mightier than John) and about baptism of the Holy Spirit are restricted to those who have been baptized.

John recognizes that his ministry is now functionally over (at least in the narrative of Markâs Gospel) and that he will no longer be baptizing. The work of ministry will pass to Jesus, as 1:14 will indicate.

The tense used here might not be particularly significant.

but he shall baptize you with the Holy Ghost: While neither the KJV nor the vast majority of ancient manuscripts include the phrase âand with fireâ in this verse, there are some manuscripts that do include it.56 Because the phrase occurs in only a few late manuscripts, it is highly unlikely to have been there originally but was probably added to conform with Matthew 3:11 and Luke 3:16.

There is no place in Markâs Gospel where Jesus does something that Mark specifically refers to as baptizing with the Holy Spirit. While modern Latter-day Saint readers would likely understand this as a reference to the gift of the Holy Spirit bestowed upon those who have recently been baptized, it is not certain that this is Johnâs meaning here; there are several possibilities for what John meant:

In Acts 2, on the Day of Pentecost, the disciples received an outpouring of the Holy Spirit. Perhaps John is prophesying of that event. Given that Jesus has already ascended into heaven at that point in Acts, however, it is somewhat awkward to describe Jesus baptizing in the Holy Spirit on the Day of Pentecost. Furthermore, it is unclear why John would use the word âbaptizeâ to describe that experience with the Spiritâwhere its use would be metaphoricalâbut then contrast it with his own baptism, which is most literal.

In Greek, the phrases âin waterâ and âin spiritâ are grammatically similar, so it is possible to understand the Spirit as a substance in which one is baptized.57Perhaps John is thinking about the Spirit as being of a substance more like a liquid or as âa cleansing agent parallel to water-baptism.â58In this reading, baptism of the Spirit would happen when the Spirit descends on Jesus after his water baptism. Yet this theory would not entirely explain Johnâs meaning, since he didnât say that Jesus would be baptized in the Spirit but rather that Jesus would baptize others in the Spiritâsomething that does not happen (at least in any obvious way) in this text.

In Joel 3:28â29, Joel prophesied of a future time when the Spirit would be âpoured outâ like water. Perhaps John was alluding to this connection between water and Spirit and implying that Jesus would be instrumental in the fulfillment of this prophecy. This association is not perfect, however, since in Joel the Spirit is âpoured out,â which is not quite the same thing as being immersed in the Spirit, as one is in a water baptism.

In the HB, only God gives the Spirit to people. So regardless of specifically how the baptism in Spirit is understood, the point of Johnâs statement is to equate Jesus with the God of the HB or, at the very least, as sharing a power normally possessed only by God.

Acts 19:1â6 addresses the difference between the baptism that John offered and receiving the Holy Spirit: Paul baptizes people in the name of Jesus (after they had already been baptized by John) and then lays his hands on their heads, which causes the Holy Spirit to come upon them. This has a strong parallel to 1:8 (as well as to modern Latter-day Saint practice), but it must be noted that there is no way to be sure that John and/or Mark shared this understanding. Further, Paulânot Jesusâis the person performing the baptisms, although he does do it in the name of Jesus.

John may be alluding to Ezekiel 36:25â28, which also mentions purification by water and the granting of the Spirit. In that passage, it is God granting the Spirit to those who are purified by water, and so this would link Jesus and God in the minds of those who heard John. This parallel is not perfect, however, because Ezekiel doesnât seem to envision a separate water baptism and spirit baptism.

Some HB passages were understood to teach that the Messiah possessed the Spirit (see 2 Sam. 23:1â2; Isa. 11:1â2; 61:1). If John is alluding to that idea, then he is identifying Jesus with the Messiah and teaching that not only will the Messiah have Godâs Spirit, but the Messiah will share it with others. (But it is also possible that there is no precedent for the idea of the Messiah bestowing the Spirit on others.)

Isaiah 32:15 states that desolation will continue âuntil a spirit from on high is poured out on us, and the wilderness becomes a fruitful field, and the fruitful field is deemed a forestâ (NRSV). Perhaps if John had this passage in mind, his wilderness location would be functioning as a metaphor for the spiritually arid state of the people until they receive the Spirit. In this view, the âSpirit baptismâ that Jesus provides is a metaphor suggesting his ability to share the Spirit with the people; a specific ordinance would not be in view.

Ezekiel 39:29 reads, âI will never again hide my face from them, when I pour out my spirit upon the house of Israel, says the Lord Godâ (NRSV). If John had this passage in mind, then he is identifying Jesus with the God of the HB and proclaiming that Jesus will be present with his people through the Spirit.

Isaiah 63:11 envisions a trek through the wilderness under the guidance of the Spirit, suggesting ample parallels with Johnâs ministry. This verse features Moses leading the people through the water and being given the Spirit. If John had this verse in mind, it would suggest not that he is differentiating his ministry from Jesusâ, but rather that he is suggesting a unity between the two ministries, since in the Isaiah reference, Moses is responsible for both the water and the Spirit.

There is no article before âholy spiritâ in the Greek text, perhaps suggesting that this is not a reference to what modern Latter-day Saints would call the third member of the Godhead but rather a generic idea of Jesus baptizing them in the Spirit (contrasted with water); this Spirit is identified as âholyâ to preempt the scribal charge that Jesus is working through an unclean spirit.

Perhaps the stronger one who is coming refers to God, since Jesus does not baptize with the Holy Spirit in Mark, but the Holy Spirit comes from heaven (Godâs realm) at Jesusâ baptism.59(One weakness of this interpretation is that the saying about loosening the sandal tie makes less sense as a reference to God than it does to Jesus.)

Given the parallels between 1:7 and 1:8, it may be better to focus on the idea that Jesusâ ministry will be superior to Johnâs ministry, without pushing the meaning of spirit baptism to any specific meaning.

John may be making a metaphorical comparison: he will immerse them in the water (in a literal way), but Jesus will immerse them in the spirit (metaphorically, through a ministry guided by and filled with the spirit).60

There is a chiastic structure as the reference to John baptizing (1:5â6) is surrounded by references to John preaching (1:4; 1:7â8). This implies a tight link between the preaching and the baptism.

Analysis

Chapter 1 is a rushing narrative that covers much ground in an amazingly short amount of time. And yet Mark is able to provide a nuanced introduction to who Jesus really is in this short text.

Markâs Gospel begins with John the Baptistâs ministryânot with Jesusâ premortal life (as Johnâs Gospel does), his genealogy (Matthew), or his motherâs and auntâs experiences (Luke). Given that discipleship will be the signal theme in the Gospel of Mark, this is the logical place for Mark to begin Jesusâ story.

Johnâs baptismal practice was, in its context, innovative in many ways. And by linking baptism and repentance, John is denying the exclusive ability of temple sacrifices to provide forgiveness, which was a challenge to the temple leadership. Additionally, Johnâs clothing and lifestyle portray him as one who rejects many social conventions. Jesusâ ministry will continue this theme of innovation.

Generally, Mark is much less concerned with showing how Jesus fulfilled HB prophecies than the other Gospel writers, especially Matthew. So it is even more striking that there are several HB references in the crucial introductory words of the text. The biblical quotations in Markâs text do three things:

God promises Jesus a preparer of his way.61The role of this preparer was important enough to have been prophesied in the HB. The inclusion of those texts also allows Markâs audience to âoverhearâ God speaking to Jesus. It suggests a very close relationship between God and Jesus.

There is a description of the preparer, as one crying in the wilderness.

There is a summary of the preparerâs message: do everything you can to make the paths accessible.62

The introduction creates a sense of âthis is the one you have been waiting for!â

Jesus does not appear in the first eight verses of the text, but Mark has introduced him through biblical prophecies and Johnâs description of the coming one. There are three comparisons that John makes between himself and Jesus: Jesus is stronger, John is not worthy to be Jesusâ slave, and Jesus offers a baptism of spirit instead of water. These three items serve to emphasize Jesusâ superiority to John, although they do not deny the possibility that Jesus was one of Johnâs disciples for a time. In fact, it is possible to read the prologue of Mark not as a description of Johnâs ministry but as an introduction to Jesus; this way of introducing Jesus makes sense in light of Markâs emphasis on discipleship, especially if Jesus was a disciple of Johnâs.

If Jesus was Johnâs disciple, then the prologue has some very interesting things to say about discipleship. While the idea of Jesus being a disciple might initially seem unusual, it fits with Markâs focus on discipleship to have Jesus introduced to the audience as a disciple. This positions him as an exemplar of discipleship for the audience. Nowhere in Markâs Gospel does Jesus command anyone to be baptized or to baptize anyone. However, Jesus does tell people to follow him, and part of his own path was being baptized by John. It makes sense to see baptism as part of the pattern of discipleship.

The audience learns from the prologue something about Markâs style. First, it is drenched in HB allusions, from actual quotations to specific allusions (mentioning Johnâs clothing to evoke Elijah) to thematic allusions (the idea of a reverse exodus or Jesus as the new creation or new Israel). These HB allusions prime Markâs audience to see that the HB promises are fulfilled in the life and ministry of Jesusâbut not in a literal or expected manner. John the Baptist does not build an actual road nor is he literally Elijah. Thus, Mark wants his audience to take the HB seriously but not literally. Mark is also allusive rather than pedantic; he presents John the Baptist dressed as Elijah instead of merely telling the audience that John was similar to Elijah. As will be shown throughout this commentary, Markâs style is a very subtle oneâmore likely to leave clues for the perceptive listener than to make proclamations.63
